A Martingale-Free Introduction to Conditional Gaussian Nonlinear Systems
Marios Andreou, Nan Chen

Abstract
The Conditional Gaussian Nonlinear System (CGNS) is a broad class of nonlinear stochastic dynamical systems. Given the trajectories for a subset of state variables, the remaining follow a Gaussian distribution. Despite the conditionally linear structure, the CGNS exhibits strong nonlinearity, thus capturing many non-Gaussian characteristics observed in nature through its joint and marginal distributions. Desirably, it enjoys closed analytic formulae for the time evolution of its conditional Gaussian statistics, which facilitate the study of data assimilation and other related topics. In this paper, we develop a martingale-free approach to improve the understanding of CGNSs. This methodology provides a tractable approach to proving the time evolution of the conditional statistics by deriving results through time discretization schemes, with the continuous-time regime obtained via a formal limiting process as the discretization time-step vanishes. This discretized approach further allows for developing analytic formulae for optimal posterior sampling of unobserved state variables with correlated noise. These tools are particularly valuable for studying extreme events and intermittency and apply to high-dimensional systems. Moreover, the approach improves the understanding of different sampling methods in characterizing uncertainty. The effectiveness of the framework is demonstrated through a physics-constrained, triad-interaction climate model with cubic nonlinearity and state-dependent cross-interacting noise.



1
Introduction

Complex turbulent nonlinear dynamical systems (CTNDSs) are prevalent in various fields such as geoscience, engineering, and material science
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
,
7
]
. These systems are characterized by high-dimensional state spaces, multiscale structures, and chaotic dynamics with positive Lyapunov exponents
[
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
,
13
]
. They often exhibit non-Gaussian features, such as intermittent extreme events and skewed probability density functions
[
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
]
. Key mathematical challenges include understanding their qualitative properties
[
2
,
3
,
18
]
, forecasting, state estimation
[
12
,
19
,
20
,
21
]
, uncertainty quantification (UQ), causal dependence
[
22
,
23
,
24
,
25
,
26
]
, developing reduced-order models
[
27
,
28
,
29
,
30
,
31
,
32
,
33
,
34
,
35
]
, and accurate prediction of their response to perturbations
[
36
,
37
,
38
,
39
]
.

Due to the lack of perfect knowledge of nature and potential computational limits, CTNDSs are often approximated with unavoidable errors
[
40
,
41
,
42
,
43
,
44
]
. State estimation is crucial for parameter estimation, prediction, and control. However, model errors in turbulent systems can be easily amplified in time. Therefore, data assimilation (DA) is widely used in practice to mitigate such errors. DA combines model information with available observations to improve state estimation. For turbulent systems, Bayesian inference is often used in DA to provide a statistical estimate known as the posterior distribution, with the model defining the so-called prior distribution while the observations construe the likelihood of the data. In many applications, observations are limited to only a subset of variables
[
12
,
45
,
19
,
20
,
21
]
. This creates additional challenges in DA, especially when inferring the unobserved variables.

Depending on the use of observations, DA methods can often be divided into two categories: filtering and smoothing. Filtering uses past observations up to the present, making it essential for initializing real-time forecast models. Smoothing, which incorporates both past and future data, provides more accurate offline estimates, particularly in reanalysis for climate and oceanography
[
46
,
47
,
48
,
49
]
. For linear systems with additive Gaussian noise, the celebrated Kalman filters and Rauch-Tung-Striebel smoothers offer closed-form solutions for the posterior distributions
[
46
,
50
,
51
,
52
]
. Beyond improving state estimation, the optimal filter and smoother statistics enable effective sampling of trajectories, offering insights beyond the point-wise estimates. Since these sampled trajectories incorporate the information from the observations, they are often more accurate than those simulated from imperfect models. These state estimation and sampling methods are widely used in practice to understand dynamical properties, develop surrogate models, assist in building stochastic parameterizations, and quantify uncertainty and model error.

Closed-form solutions for DA are rarely available for general CTNDSs due to their nonlinear dynamics and non-Gaussian statistics, especially for sampling and estimating the latent dynamics. As a result, numerical methods such as the ensemble Kalman filter (EnKF) and particle filter have been developed for nonlinear or non-Gaussian systems
[
20
,
21
,
53
,
54
,
55
]
, along with their smoother counterparts
[
56
,
57
]
. These methods are widely used across various scientific disciplines and are essential for ensemble forecasting in operational systems
[
58
,
59
,
60
]
. However, simulating high-dimensional CTNDSs is computationally expensive, limiting the number of ensemble members or particles that can be used, which can introduce a nontrivial bias and instability in the state estimates (such as finite-time blowup of the
solution)
[
61
,
62
]
, with the number of samples needed to address these stability concerns increasing at an exponential rate
[
63
]
. Techniques like noise inflation, localization, and resampling help address these issues, but they are often empirical and lack natural integration into the state estimation framework
[
53
,
64
,
65
,
66
]
. Furthermore, as inherently approximate in nature, the careful study of the accuracy of these pro tem approaches is warranted. Thus, closed-form analytic methods are preferred for enhancing not only the computational efficiency and
numerical behavior of probabilistic state estimation, but also in promoting the accurate capture of the salient non-Gaussian features of the dynamics, e.g., intermittent extreme events, and also in facilitating the theoretical study of the model error, as well as the uncertainty in the estimated state.

Instead of modifying DA schemes directly, computational challenges in state estimation can be addressed by developing approximate models with analytic solutions for posterior distributions while retaining key system characteristics. Linearizing strongly nonlinear systems can introduce significant errors in the mean’s and higher-order moments’ time evolution, but a special class of systems, known as conditional Gaussian nonlinear systems (CGNSs), offers a better alternative
[
67
,
68
,
69
,
70
,
71
]
. Despite the intrinsic nonlinearity and non-Gaussian statistics, the conditional distribution of the unobserved variables given one time series of the observed variables is Gaussian. This conditional Gaussianity provides closed-form solutions for the optimal filter and smoother statistics, which facilitate efficient and accurate state estimation without empirical tuning. The CGNS framework has been successfully applied in DA, prediction, and machine learning, offering analytic tools for performance assessment and error quantification
[
67
,
72
,
73
,
74
]
.

This paper aims to introduce the CGNS framework without relying on martingale theory
[
69
]
to establish the conditional Gaussianity of the posterior distribution or to derive optimal nonlinear state estimation equations. This approach unifies discrete- and continuous-time formulations, which are often treated separately, by showing that the discrete system can converge to its continuous counterpart under appropriate consistency and stability conditions. In addition to state estimation through filtering and smoothing, we explore the conditional sampling of the unobserved variables when correlated noise appears. These sampled trajectories, similar to ensemble members in the ensemble DA, preserve temporal dependencies. The temporal correlation distinguishes them from time series formed by independent posterior samples at different instants. The sampling schemes are derived using forward (filter-based) and backward (smoother-based) methods, with closed-form solutions provided for both. This facilitates systematically comparing these methods in capturing nonlinear and non-Gaussian features associated with the unobserved states, especially for intermittent behaviors and extreme events. The sampled trajectories further highlight the limitations of treating the posterior mean estimates as surrogates of the hidden variables. Finally, we discuss a hierarchy that emerges in the fluctuation component of the sample-to-sample uncertainty, which decreases as more information from the observable process is incorporated, depending on whether the filter- or smoother-based sampling methodology is being used.

The remainder of this work is organized as follows. Section
2
presents the CGNS modeling framework and derives the optimal nonlinear filter and smoother state estimation equations using a martingale-free approach. In Section
3
, we construct the filter-based forward and smoother-based backward sampling procedures for the hidden process and demonstrate their first- and second-order moment consistency with the Gaussian statistics of the optimal posterior distributions from Section
2
. We also compare their dynamical properties in terms of their uncertainty, specifically through their damping and fluctuation constituents, both together and with those from the unconditional forward run of the system. An illustrative numerical experiment in Section
4
highlights the differences between the optimal nonlinear filter and smoother, focusing on effective state estimation and sampling of the hidden intermittent time series. The article concludes in Section
5
. The Appendix includes the details of the proofs of most theorems in this work.

2
The Conditional Gaussian Nonlinear System Modeling Framework

2.1
Basic Concepts

Throughout this paper, boldface letters are exclusively used to denote vectors for mathematical clarity. Lowercase boldface letters represent column vectors, while uppercase boldface letters denote matrices. The only exception is
\mathbf{W}
(with subscripts or superscripts), which denotes a Wiener process. Although this always corresponds to a column vector in this work, we use an uppercase letter due to literary convention.

Let
t
denote the time variable, with
t\in[0,T]
, where
T>0
may be infinite. Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
be a complete probability space, and let
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
be a filtration of sub-
\sigma
-algebras of
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
. We assume this filtration is augmented (i.e., complete and right-continuous), forming the stochastic basis
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]},\mathbb{P})
. For any filtration, there exists a smallest augmented filtration refining
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
(known as its completion), so this is without loss of generality. We denote the partially observable
(S,\mathcal{A})
-valued stochastic process as
(\mathbf{x}(t,\omega),\mathbf{y}(t,\omega))
for
t\in[0,T]
and
\omega\in\Omega
. Here,
\mathbf{x}
is the observable component, while
\mathbf{y}
is the unobservable component. The theory that follows can be applied mutatis mutandis to any partially observable stochastic process that takes values over a measurable space
(S,\mathcal{A})
, where
S
is a separable Hilbert space (finite-dimensional or not) over a complete scalar ground field, and
\mathcal{A}
is a
\sigma
-algebra on
S
. For this work, we consider complex-valued finite-dimensional processes with respect to the usual Euclidean inner product. Thus, we set
S=\mathbb{C}^{k+l}
and
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{C}^{k+l}}\equiv\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{R}^{2(k+l)}}
, with
\mathbf{x}
as a
k
-dimensional vector and
\mathbf{y}
as an
l
-dimensional vector. Here,
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{R}^{2(k+l)}}
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathbb{R}^{2(k+l)}
, since
\mathrm{dim}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{C}^{k+l})=2(k+l)
. We assume that

(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\left((x_{1}(t),\ldots,x_{k}(t),y_{1}(t),\ldots,y_{l}(t)),\mathcal{F}_{t}\right),\quad t\in[0,T],

indicating that the partially observable random process is (jointly) adapted (or non-anticipative) to the filtration
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
. This means that for all times
t\in[0,T]
, the random vector defined by
\left(\mathbf{x}(t,\cdot)^{\mathtt{T}},\mathbf{y}(t,\cdot)^{\mathtt{T}}\right)^{\mathtt{T}}:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}^{k+l}
is an
(\mathcal{F}_{t};\mathcal{A})
-measurable function. Specifically, this implies that the natural filtration
\mathcal{F}
with respect to
\{\mathbf{x}(s)\}_{s\leq t}
, which is the sub-
\sigma
-algebra generated by the observable processes for times
s\leq t
, is defined as

\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\mathbf{x}}:=\sigma\left(\{\mathbf{x}(s)\}_{s\leq t}\right)=\left\{\mathbf{x}(s)^{-1}[A]=\mathbf{x}(s,\cdot)^{-1}[A]:A\in\mathcal{A},\,s\leq t\right\}.

This satisfies
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\mathbf{x}}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{t}
, since
\mathbf{x}
is adapted to the filtration
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
by construction. By definition,
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\mathbf{x}}
is the smallest such filtration. We refer to this natural filtration as the observable
\sigma
-algebra (at time
t
) for the remainder of this work.

It can be shown that, at each time instant
t\in[0,T]
, the optimal estimate in the mean-square sense, which minimizes the expected squared norm error of some
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable function
h(t,\mathbf{x}(t),\mathbf{y}(t))
of
t
,
\mathbf{x}
, and
\mathbf{y}
, based on the observations up to time
t
,
\{\mathbf{x}(s)\}_{s\leq t}
, is exactly its conditional expectation conditioned on the observable
\sigma
-algebra at time
t
, or
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{h}\big|\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\mathbf{x}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{h}(t,\mathbf{x}(t),\mathbf{y}(t))\big|\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\mathbf{x}}\right]
. This is known as the a-posteriori mean, and its optimality rests on the tacit assumption that
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{h}(t,\mathbf{x}(t),\mathbf{y}(t))\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]
is finite, where
\left\|\boldsymbol{\cdot}\right\|_{2}
denotes the usual Euclidean norm over
\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{h})}

[
68
,
69
]
. This stochastic functional is to be understood as the up to a
\mathbb{P}
-null set unique integrable
\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{h})}
-valued and
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\mathbf{x}}
-measurable random vector satisfying

\int_{F}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{h}\big|\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\mathbf{x}}\right]\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}=\int_{F}\mathbf{h}\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P},\quad\forall F\in\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\mathbf{x}},

with the existence and uniqueness (up to
\mathbb{P}
-null sets) of the a-posteriori mean being provided by the Hilbert projection theorem. This means that the random vector such that the residual
\mathbf{h}-\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{h}\big|\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\mathbf{x}}\right]
is orthogonal to the indicator functions
\mathds{1}_{F}
under the Euclidean inner product over
L^{2}\left(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{t},\mathbb{P};\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{h})}\right)
, or the Lebesgue quotient space of square-integrable processes taking values in
\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{h})}
, modulo the adapted processes with a squared null integral
\forall F\in\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\mathbf{x}}
w.r.t.
\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}
, i.e.,
\int_{F}\|\mathbf{b}(t)\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}=0
. Usually
\mathbf{h}
is mainly a function of the unobserved process
\mathbf{y}
, and in this work we exclusively use
\mathbf{h}=\mathbf{y}
to recover the optimal filter and smoother conditional Gaussian statistics of the hidden process when conditioning on the observations.

The goal in optimal state estimation is to characterize the a-posteriori mean by a system of stochastic differential equations (SDEs) called the optimal nonlinear filter (state estimation) equations or optimal nonlinear smoother (state estimation) equations, depending on whether we condition up to the current observation or the whole observational period (in the case where an observed time series exists a-priori), respectively. In general, without any special assumptions on the structure of the processes
\mathbf{h}
and
\mathbf{x}
,
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{h}\big|\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\mathbf{x}}\right]
is difficult to determine. However, under the assumption that their components are of a specific type, then we can characterize the a-posteriori mean through a system of analytic equations. Specifically, in the most general setting, such a system of analytic equations can be recovered under certain regularity assumptions, which include the
\mathbf{h}=(\mathbf{h}(t,\mathbf{x}(t),\mathbf{y}(t)),\mathcal{F}_{t})
process,
t\leq T
, being represented as

\mathbf{h}(t,\mathbf{x}(t),\mathbf{y}(t))=\mathbf{h}(0,\mathbf{x}(0),\mathbf{y}(0))+\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\xi}(s,\mathbf{x}(s),\mathbf{y}(s))\mathrm{d}s+\mathbf{z}(t,\mathbf{x}(t),\mathbf{y}(t)),

(2.1)

where
\mathbf{z}=(\mathbf{z}(t,\mathbf{x}(t),\mathbf{y}(t)),\mathcal{F}_{t})
is a martingale and
\boldsymbol{\xi}=(\boldsymbol{\xi}(t,\mathbf{x}(t),\mathbf{y}(t)),\mathcal{F}_{t})
is a nonanticipative process such that it is
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. (almost surely) in an appropriate
L^{2}
Bochner space, i.e.,
\mathbb{P}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}(s,\mathbf{x}(s),\mathbf{y}(s))\right\|_{2}\mathrm{d}s<+\infty\right)=1
, while the observable process is assumed to be an Itô process of the diffusive type, i.e.,

\mathbf{x}(t)=\mathbf{x}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{a}(s,\mathbf{x}(s))\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{B}(s,\mathbf{x}(s))\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}(s),

(2.2)

where
\mathbf{W}=(\mathbf{W}(t),\mathcal{F}_{t})
is a
d
-dimensional complex-valued Wiener process, and the processes
\mathbf{a}(t,\mathbf{x}(t))\in\mathbb{C}^{k}
and
\mathbf{B}(t,\mathbf{x}(t))\in\mathbb{C}^{k\times d}
are nonanticipative (at least for almost all times
t\in[0,T]
), with the former being (Lebesgue) integrable while the latter is
\mathbf{W}
-integrable,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s
[
68
]
. Unfortunately, under this general formulation, one encounters the inherent difficulty of needing to know the higher-order conditional moments of the desired stochastic functional
\mathbf{h}
, specifically the ratios between them (in the tesnor sense), as to be able to arrive to a closed system of explicitly solvable equations for the a-posteriori mean (or covariance).

Nonetheless, for a broad class of nonlinear systems, that being the class of CGNSs, the challenge of non-closedness can be effectively addressed to yield a closed system of optimal nonlinear posterior state estimation equations, as discussed in Chapter 12 of Liptser & Shiryayev
[
69
]
. The CGNS framework is particularly powerful in this context, as it provides a closed system of equations for the a-posteriori mean, stemming from the conditional Gaussianity of the posterior distributions. This property allows for the expression of higher-order conditional moments solely in terms of the lower-order ones, specifically through the conditional mean and covariance tensor, thanks to the conditional Gaussian structure.

2.2
Conditionally Gaussian Nonlinear Systems

In its most general form, a conditional Gaussian system of processes consists of two diffusion-type processes defined by the following system of stochastic differentials given in Itô form

[
69
,
71
,
75
]
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}(t)

\displaystyle=\left[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{x})\mathbf{y}(t)+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{x})\right]\mathrm{d}t+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_{1}(t)+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_{2}(t),

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}(t)

\displaystyle=\left[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y}}(t,\mathbf{x})\mathbf{y}(t)+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y}}(t,\mathbf{x})\right]\mathrm{d}t+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{y}}(t,\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_{1}(t)+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{y}}(t,\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_{2}(t),

(2.4)

where

\mathbf{W}_{1}=\big((W_{11}(t),\ldots,W_{1d}(t)),\mathcal{F}_{t}\big)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{W}_{2}=\big((W_{21}(t),\ldots,W_{2r}(t)),\mathcal{F}_{t}\big),

are two mutually independent complex-valued Wiener processes (i.e., both their real and imaginary parts are mutually independent real-valued Wiener processes) and almost every path of
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
is in
C^{0}([0,T];\mathbb{C}^{k})
and
C^{0}([0,T];\mathbb{C}^{l})
, respectively. The elements of the vector- and matrix-valued functions of multiplicative factors (
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}},\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y}}
), forcings (
\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x}},\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y}}
), and noise feedbacks (
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{x}},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{x}},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{y}},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{y}}
) are assumed to be nonanticipative (adapted) functionals over the measurable time-function cylinder

(C^{0,k}_{T},\mathscr{B}^{k}_{T}):=\big([0,T]\times C^{0}([0,T];\mathbb{C}^{k}),\mathcal{B}([0,T])\otimes\mathcal{B}(C^{0}([0,T];\mathbb{C}^{k}))\big),

where
\otimes
denotes the tensor-product
\sigma
-algebra on the underlying product space, i.e.,

\mathscr{B}^{k}_{T}=\mathcal{B}([0,T])\otimes\mathcal{B}(C^{0}([0,T];\mathbb{C}^{k}))=\sigma\left(\big\{A\times B:A\in\mathcal{B}([0,T]),B\in\mathcal{B}(C^{0}([0,T];\mathbb{C}^{k}))\big\}\right),

with
\mathcal{B}(C^{0}([0,T];\mathbb{C}^{k}))
being a
\sigma
-algebra generated by some topology on the space of continuous functions from
[0,T]
to
\mathbb{C}^{k}
,
C^{0}([0,T];\mathbb{C}^{k})
(e.g., the Isbell topology or the
\sigma
-algebra generated by equipping the space of continuous functions with the compact-open topology, which simplifies to the topology of compact convergence, thus making the space Polish and reducing
\mathcal{B}(C^{0}([0,T];\mathbb{C}^{k}))
to the
\sigma
-algebra of Borelian subsets
[
76
,
77
]
). This means that if
g(t,\mathbf{x})
denotes a standard element of these functionals, for each
t\in[0,T]
, it is
(\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\mathbf{x}};\mathbb{C})
-measurable. Observe how this is a weaker assumption imposed on the model parameters compared to progressive joint measurability, i.e., where each one of their standard elements is instead
(\mathcal{B}([0,t])\otimes\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\mathbf{x}};\mathbb{C})
-measurable for every
t\in[0,T]

[
78
]
. It is important to emphasize here the fact that in a CGNS, the unobservable component
\mathbf{y}
enters into (
2.3
)–(
2.4
) in a purely linear manner, whereas the observable process
\mathbf{x}
can enter into the coefficients of both equations in any
\mathcal{F}^{\mathbf{x}}_{t}
-measurable way, by allowing the model components
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}
,
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y}}
,
\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x}}
,
\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y}}
,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}_{1}
,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}_{2}
,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}_{1}
, and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}_{2}
to be nonlinear functionals of the known components of the state variable.

2.3
Significance and Broad Applications of CGNSs

A variety of CTNDSs can be represented within the CGNS framework. Notable examples include physics-constrained nonlinear stochastic models, such as noisy Lorenz models
[
79
,
80
]
, low-order representations of Charney-DeVore flows
[
81
]
, and models capturing topographic mean flow interactions
[
13
,
82
]
. Additionally, stochastically coupled reaction-diffusion models are prevalent in fields like neuroscience and ecology, including models like the stochastically coupled FitzHugh-Nagumo
[
83
,
84
,
83
,
84
]
, stochastically forced predator-prey models
[
85
,
86
]
, and SIR epidemic models
[
87
,
88
]
. Furthermore, multiscale models for geophysical flows, such as the Boussinesq equations with added noise
[
89
,
90
,
91
]
and the stochastically forced rotating shallow water equation
[
92
]
, also fall within this framework
[
71
]
. This modeling approach has been applied to describe many natural phenomena as well, such as the Madden-Julian oscillation and Arctic sea ice dynamics
[
73
,
93
]
.

In addition to modeling a plethora of complex phenomena, the CGNS framework and its closed analytic DA formulae have been utilized to answer many theoretical questions and design computational strategies. Specifically, the classical Kalman-Bucy filter
[
50
]
serves as a fundamental special case of a CGNS DA scheme. But beyond this, the CGNSs has been utilized to develop an exact nonlinear Lagrangian DA algorithm that advances rigorous analysis to understand model errors and uncertainties in state recovery
[
74
,
94
,
95
]
. This includes the usage of the framework not just for high-dimensional state estimation of ocean flows, but also in determining the optimal launching locations for Lagrangian tracers under uncertainty
[
96
,
97
]
. Furthermore, the analytically solvable DA schemes have been applied for state estimation and the prediction of intermittent time series associated with monsoons and other climatic events
[
75
,
98
]
. Moreover, the effective DA procedures have contributed to developing rapid algorithms for addressing high-dimensional Fokker-Planck equations
[
99
,
100
]
.

Importantly, the underlying principles of designing CGNSs have been extended to much broader applications. Examples include the development of forecasting models within the realm of dynamic stochastic superresolution
[
101
,
102
]
, the creation of stochastic superparameterizations for geophysical turbulence
[
103
,
104
,
105
]
, and the design of efficient multiscale DA strategies
[
106
,
107
]
. It has also been used for the development of efficient data-driven multiscale reduced-order, as well as deep learning,
modeling frameworks for CTNDSs
[
108
,
109
]
.

The CGNS framework differs significantly from other closure modeling approaches, such as linear regression models
[
110
,
111
]
and projection methods like the Galerkin proper orthogonal decomposition
[
112
,
113
,
114
]
. These methods typically create surrogate models that are more computationally efficient than the original, addressing truncation errors through closure terms. In contrast, closure models for the large-scale variable
\mathbf{x}
do not incorporate the small-scale variables
\mathbf{y}
, relying instead on ”diagnostic” terms that parameterize the nonlinear coupling between slow and fast variables. These terms often involve past values of
\mathbf{x}
and are derived from the Mori-Zwanzig (MZ) formalism from statistical mechanics
[
115
,
116
,
117
,
118
]
. The CGNS, however, employs a simplified prognostic equation for the unresolved small-scale variables
\mathbf{y}
, allowing them to influence the dynamics only linearly, while permitting nonlinear dependencies in the coefficients of drift and diffusion.

A treatise on the broad applications of the CGNS framework in the prediction, state estimation, and uncertainty quantification of multiscale nonlinear stochastic systems is provided in Chen and Majda
[
71
]
.

2.4
Preliminaries

Let the following denote the standard elements of the vector- and matrix-valued functionals appearing in (
2.3
)–(
2.4
) as model parameters:

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{x}):=(f^{\mathbf{x}}_{1}(t,x),\ldots,f^{\mathbf{x}}_{k}(t,x))^{\mathtt{T}},\quad\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y}}(t,\mathbf{x}):=(f^{\mathbf{y}}_{1}(t,x),\ldots,f^{\mathbf{y}}_{l}(t,x))^{\mathtt{T}},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{x}):=\left(\Lambda^{\mathbf{x}}_{ij}(t,\mathbf{x})\right)_{k\times l},\quad\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{x}):=\left(\Sigma^{\mathbf{x},1}_{ij}(t,\mathbf{x})\right)_{k\times d},\quad\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{x}):=\left(\Sigma^{\mathbf{x},2}_{ij}(t,\mathbf{x})\right)_{k\times r},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y}}(t,\mathbf{x}):=\left(\Lambda^{\mathbf{y}}_{ij}(t,\mathbf{x})\right)_{l\times l},\quad\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{y}}(t,\mathbf{x}):=\left(\Sigma^{\mathbf{y},1}_{ij}(t,\mathbf{x})\right)_{l\times d},\quad\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{y}}(t,\mathbf{x}):=\left(\Sigma^{\mathbf{y},2}_{ij}(t,\mathbf{x})\right)_{l\times r}.

As to be able to obtain the main results which define the CGNS framework and its potency in DA through closed-form expressions for the posterior statistics, a set of sufficient regularity conditions needs to be assumed a-priori. For the following conditions, each respective pair of indices
i
and
j
takes all admissible values and
\mathbf{z}
is a
k
-dimensional function in
C^{0}([0,T];\mathbb{C}^{k})
:

(1)

The multiplicative factor matrix and forcing vector in the unobservable process are Lebesgue integrable, while all noise feedback matrices and multiplicative factor matrix and forcing vector in the observable process are
\mathbf{W}
-integrable (in the Itô sense), or equivalently, by Itô isometry,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\Big[

\displaystyle|f^{\mathbf{y}}_{i}(t,\mathbf{z})|+|\Lambda^{\mathbf{y}}_{ij}(t,\mathbf{z})|+\big|f^{\mathbf{x}}_{i}(t,\mathbf{z})\big|^{2}+\big|\Lambda^{\mathbf{x}}_{ij}(t,\mathbf{z})\big|^{2}

\displaystyle+\big|\Sigma^{\mathbf{x},1}_{ij}(t,\mathbf{z})\big|^{2}+\big|\Sigma^{\mathbf{x},2}_{ij}(t,\mathbf{z})\big|^{2}+\big|\Sigma^{\mathbf{y},1}_{ij}(t,\mathbf{z})\big|^{2}+\big|\Sigma^{\mathbf{y},2}_{ij}(t,\mathbf{z})\big|^{2}\Big]\mathrm{d}t<+\infty.

This ensures the existence of the integrals in (
2.3
)–(
2.4
)
[
68
,
69
]
.

(2)

\displaystyle|\Lambda^{\mathbf{x}}_{ij}(t,\mathbf{z})|,|\Lambda^{\mathbf{y}}_{ij}(t,\mathbf{z})|\leq L_{1}
for some (uniform)
L_{1}>0
,
\forall t\in[0,T]
.

(3)

If
g(t,\mathbf{z})
denotes any element of the multiplicative factors in the drift dynamics,
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}
and
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y}}
, or of the noise feedback matrices,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{m}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{z})
and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{m}^{\mathbf{y}}(t,\mathbf{z})
for
m=1,2
, and
K(s)
is a nondecreasing right-continuous function taking values in
[0,1]
, then there
\exists L_{2},L_{3},L_{4},L_{5}>0
such that for any
\mathbf{z}
and
\mathbf{w}

k
-dimensional functions in
C^{0}([0,T];\mathbb{C}^{k})
we have

\displaystyle|g(t,\mathbf{z})-g(t,\mathbf{w})|^{2}\leq L_{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\mathbf{z}(s)-\mathbf{w}(s)\right\|_{2}^{2}\mathrm{d}K(s)+L_{3}\left\|\mathbf{z}(t)-\mathbf{w}(t)\right\|_{2}^{2},\ \forall t\in[0,T],

\displaystyle|g(t,\mathbf{z})|^{2}\leq L_{4}\int_{0}^{t}(1+\left\|\mathbf{z}(s)\right\|_{2}^{2})\mathrm{d}K(s)+L_{5}(1+\left\|\mathbf{z}(t)\right\|_{2}^{2}),\ \forall t\in[0,T].

By the properties of
K
, the integrals are to be understood in the Lebesgue–Stieltjes integration sense. The first inequality acts as a generalized or weak global Lipschitz condition, while the second one establishes an at most linear growth condition, both in terms of the spatial component (which can be weakened to local boundedness or local integrability of the drift components and to Sobolev diffusion elements for existence of a unique local strong solution
[
119
,
120
]
).

(4)

\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathbf{x}(0)\|_{2}^{2}+\|\mathbf{y}(0)\|_{2}^{2}\right]<+\infty
. This also implies
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. finiteness of the initial conditions by Markov’s inequality.

\blacktriangleright

Assumptions
(1)
–
(4)
are sufficient in establishing the existence and uniqueness (both in the strong (pathwise) and weak (in law) sense) of a strong solution to the CGNS of SDEs that is continuous in
t
, continuously depends on the initial distributions and additional model parameters, and satisfies
\underset{t\in[0,T]}{\sup}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathbf{x}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+\|\mathbf{y}(t)\|_{2}^{2}\right]\right\}<+\infty

[
121
,
122
,
123
,
124
,
125
]
. A proof of this specific fact is given in Theorem 12.4 of Liptser & Shiryaev
[
69
]
for when
k=l=1
, which can be easily adapted to the multi-dimensional case for arbitrary
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
(see also
[
126
]
). These results also extend to the existence and uniqueness of solutions, that are also continuous in
t
and continuously depend on the initial data and additional model parameters, to the optimal nonlinear filter and smoother state estimation equations, as well as associated sampling SDEs, which are provided in Sections
2.5
and
3.1
–
3.2
, respectively (see note accompanying assumptions
(5)
–
(8)
). Note that the globality of the Lipschitz conditions in
(3)
is unavoidable, since from classical SDE theory, while local Lipschitzness (in space, uniformly in time) guarantees strong uniqueness, it can only ensure existence up to the ”blow-up time” of the local solution
[
78
]
. Notably, in the case where
(3)
holds solely for the observable diffusion components
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}_{m}
, with
m=1,2
, then this is sufficient for the existence and uniqueness of continuous solutions to the optimal nonlinear filter and smoother state estimation equations (and associated sampling formulae), granted that there preexists a strong local solution to the CGNS of equations, as it leads to the unique strong solution of an auxiliary SDE which is necessary in establishing a certain equivalence of probability measures and to give a well-defined Bayes formula
[
126
]
.

(5)

The sum of the Gramians (with respect to rows) of the noise coefficient matrices in the observable process are uniformly nonsingular, i.e., the elements of the inverse of

(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})(t,\mathbf{z})=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{z})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{z})^{\dagger}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{z})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{z})^{\dagger},

are uniformly bounded in
[0,T]
, where
\boldsymbol{\cdot}^{\dagger}
denotes the Hermitian transpose operator. This ensures the nondegeneracy of stochastic measures associated with
\mathbf{x}
, as well as the invertibility of the observable Gramian over time.

(6)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[|\Lambda^{\mathbf{x}}_{ij}(t,\mathbf{z}(t))y_{j}(t)|\right]\mathrm{d}t<+\infty
.

(7)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|y_{j}(t)|\right]<+\infty,\ t\in[0,T]
.

(8)

For
\mu_{\text{f},j}(t):=\mathbb{E}\left[y_{j}(t)\big|\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\mathbf{x}}\right]
, where
t\in[0,T]
and
j=1,\ldots,l
, we assume that for all
i=1,\ldots,k
and
j=1,\ldots,l
we have:

\mathbb{P}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\left|\Lambda^{\mathbf{x}}_{ij}(t,\mathbf{z}(t))\mu_{\text{f},j}(t)\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}t<+\infty\right)=1.

\blacktriangleright

While assumptions
(1)
–
(4)
are there to guarantee existence and uniqueness of a continuous strong solution to the CGNS of SDEs, the additional assumptions
(5)
–
(8)
extend these properties to solutions of the optimal nonlinear posterior state estimation equations, and combined with the previous conditions they are additionally sufficient in achieving the stochastic stability (e.g., in the mean-square sense)
[
127
,
128
,
129
,
130
,
131
,
132
]
, as well as the strong and weak consistency of temporally discretized numerical integration schemes applied to (
2.3
)–(
2.4
), like those of Euler-Maruyama (EM) and Milstein
[
133
,
134
]
, for when the limit of the maximum width of the time subintervals goes to zero. These conditions are known to be equivalent to strong and weak convergence, respectively, through an appropriate stochastic version of the Lax equivalence theorem
[
133
,
135
]
.

We note here that the conditions
(1)
–
(8)
assumed here, are only sufficient, with the possibility of some of them being slightly weakened, as already discussed (see the works of Kolodziej et al. for the CGNS framework specifically
[
126
,
136
]
).

Contingent on this set of sufficient assumptions, it is then possible to show that the posterior distribution of the unobserved variables, when conditioning on the observational data, is Gaussian. This is exactly the reason why the system in (
2.3
)–(
2.4
) is called a conditionally Gaussian dynamical system of equations. For the remainder of this work, as to make our notation more explicit, we write
\big(\boldsymbol{\cdot}\big|\mathbf{x}(s),s\leq t\big)
to indicate the fact that we are conditioning on the observable
\sigma
-algebra at time
t
,
\big(\boldsymbol{\cdot}\big|\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\mathbf{x}}\big)
(and likewise for when conditioning on other (sub-)
\sigma
-algebras, unless otherwise noted). Strictly speaking this is an abuse of notation since it is not correct to condition on an explicit path but instead the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the state variable, specifically the observable filtration in this case
[
125
]
. Nonetheless, this abuse of notation is adopted for notational simplicity and didactic reasons.

Theorem 2.1

(
Conditional Gaussianity
)
.

Let
\mathbf{x}(t)
and
\mathbf{y}(t)
satisfy (
2.3
)–(
2.4
) and assume that the regularity conditions
(1)
–
(8)
hold. Additionally, assume that the initial conditional distribution
\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}(0)\leq\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}\big|\mathbf{x}(0)\big)
1
1

1

The event
\{\mathbf{y}(s)\leq\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{l})^{\mathtt{T}}\}
is to be understood coordinate-wise, i.e.,
\{y_{1}(s)\leq\alpha_{1},\ldots,y_{l}(s)\leq\alpha_{l}\}
, with the linear partial ordering assumed on
\mathbb{C}
, i.e.,
y_{j}(s)\leq\alpha_{j}\Leftrightarrow\big(\mathrm{Re}(y_{j}(s))<\mathrm{Re}(\alpha_{j})\big)\vee\big(\mathrm{Re}(y_{j}(s))=\mathrm{Re}(\alpha_{j})\wedge\mathrm{Im}(y_{j}(s))\leq\mathrm{Im}(\alpha_{j})\big)
. (Note that this ordering is still not compatible with multiplication, since
\mathbb{C}
is algebraically closed thus prohibiting it from being an ordered field.) This ordering is adopted for the remainder of this work.
be
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. Gaussian,
\mathcal{N}_{l}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}(0),\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(0))
, and mutually independent from the Wiener processes
\mathbf{W}_{1}
and
\mathbf{W}_{2}
, where

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}(0):=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{y}(0)\big|\mathbf{x}(0)\right]\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(0):=\mathbb{E}\left[(\mathbf{y}(0)-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}(0))(\mathbf{y}(0)-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}(0))^{\dagger}\big|\mathbf{x}(0)\right].

Furthermore, assume
\mathbb{P}\left(\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(0))<+\infty\right)=1
, where
\mathrm{tr}(\boldsymbol{\cdot})
denotes the trace operator, meaning the initial estimation mean-square error between
\mathbf{y}(0)
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}(0)
is almost surely finite. Then, for any set of
\{t_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq n}
such that
0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<\cdots<t_{n}\leq t
, with
t\in[0,T]
, and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{n}\in\mathbb{C}^{l}
, the conditional distribution

\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}(t_{1})\leq\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}(t_{n})\leq\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{n}\big|\mathbf{x}(s),s\leq t\big),

is
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. Gaussian.

Proof of Theorem
2.1
.

This is Theorem 12.6 in Liptser & Shiryaev
[
69
]
, which is the multi-dimensional analog of Theorem 11.1. For the analogous result in the case of discrete time, see Theorem 13.3, with the respective sufficient assumptions given in Subchapter 13.2.1. Thorough details are also provided in Kolodziej
[
126
]
for the continuous-time case. The proof, regardless of continuous- or discrete-time, uses the conditional characteristic function method and a conditional version of the law-uniqueness theorem
[
126
,
137
]
, and is independent of the discretization-based martingale-free proof framework adopted in this work.
∎

2.5
Analytically Solvable Filter and Smoother Posterior Distributions

Before proceeding, we first make some notational clarifications. The big-O notation has its usual meaning of describing the limiting behavior of a scalar, vector, or matrix expression. Specifically, if
\mathbf{f}
is either a scalar-, vector-, or matrix-valued function with
\|\boldsymbol{\cdot}\|
being an appropriate norm over its range, then for
g
being a strictly positive scalar function over the positive reals, we have that

\mathbf{f}(\Delta t)=O(g(\Delta t))\ (\text{as }\Delta t\to 0^{+})\overset{\text{def}}{\Longleftrightarrow}\limsup_{\Delta t\to 0^{+}}\frac{\|\mathbf{f}(\Delta t)\|}{g(\Delta t)}<+\infty,

where
\Delta t
, in this work, exclusively describes the mesh or norm (length of the longest subinterval) of a partition over the time interval of interest. Additionally, if
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{C}^{k}
and
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{C}^{l}
are two random vectors, then
\mathrm{Cov}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w})=\overline{\mathbf{z}^{\prime}(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})^{\dagger}}\in\mathbb{C}^{k\times l}
is their cross-covariance matrix, while
\mathrm{Var}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w})\in\mathbb{C}^{(k+l)\times(k+l)}
is their covariance matrix, which when decomposed into a
2\times 2
block matrix, its
(1,2)
block is exactly
\mathrm{Cov}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w})
, while its
(2,1)
block is
\mathrm{Cov}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w})^{\dagger}=\mathrm{Cov}(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{z})\in\mathbb{C}^{l\times k}
. Essentially, with this notation, we have that
\mathrm{Var}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w})\equiv\mathrm{Cov}((\mathbf{z}^{\mathtt{T}},\mathbf{w}^{\mathtt{T}})^{\mathtt{T}},(\mathbf{z}^{\mathtt{T}},\mathbf{w}^{\mathtt{T}})^{\mathtt{T}})
. Finally, for
A(\lambda)
and
B(\lambda)
being two families of objects parameterized by
\lambda\in\Lambda
, then
A\lesssim B
is equivalent to there
\exists C>0
such that
\forall\lambda\in\Lambda
we have
A(\lambda)\leq CB(\lambda)
. This aids in simplifying our notation and relieving from the need of keeping track of constant multiplicative factors in inequalities.

In what follows, we establish the time-discretization setup which is utilized throughout this work. Specifically, we define the EM discretization of the CGNS in (
2.3
)–(
2.4
) and the associated continuous-time extended EM discrete approximation which recovers the strong solution to the CGNS under the expected mean-square metric up to first-order, i.e.,
O(\Delta t)
(see foregoing discussion). We first start by ”compactifying” the CGNS in (
2.3
)–(
2.4
), which we represent via the formal integral equation

\mathbf{v}(t)=\mathbf{v}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{f}(s,\mathbf{v}(s))\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}(s,\mathbf{v}(s))\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}(s),

(2.5)

for

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}(t):=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{x}(t)\\
\mathbf{y}(t)\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{W}(t):=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{W}_{1}(t)\\
\mathbf{W}_{2}(t)\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{f}(t,\mathbf{v}):=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}_{k\times k}&\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{x})\\
\mathbf{0}_{l\times k}&\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y}}(t,\mathbf{x})\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{v}+\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{x})\\
\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y}}(t,\mathbf{x})\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Sigma}(t,\mathbf{v}):=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{x})&\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{x})\\
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{y}}(t,\mathbf{x})&\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{y}}(t,\mathbf{x})\end{pmatrix}.

As aforementioned, the discrete-time approximation of
\mathbf{v}
adopted in this work, which is coincidentally the simplest one used for Monte Carlo approximation of (
2.5
), and also recovers its strong solution in the limit, is given by the explicit EM scheme that is defined as the discretized process

\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{\Delta t}^{j+1}=\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{\Delta t}^{j}+\mathbf{f}(t_{j},\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{\Delta t}^{j})(t_{j+1}-t_{j})+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}(t_{j},\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{\Delta t}^{j})\sqrt{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}^{j},\ j=0,1,\ldots,J-1,\quad\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{\Delta t}^{0}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\mathbf{v}(0),

(2.6)

where
\{t_{j}\}_{0\leq j\leq J}
is a partition of
[0,T]
with
t_{0}=0
,
t_{n}=T
, and norm or mesh given by
\Delta t=\underset{0\leq j\leq J-1}{\max}\{\Delta t_{j}\}
for
\Delta t_{j}=t_{j+1}-t_{j}
, and
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}^{j}
are
(k+l)
-dimensional complex standard normal vectors that are mutually independent from each other, as well as from the initial distribution of the state variables,
\mathbf{v}(0)
. For simplicity, we from now on assume that this temporal partition is uniform, i.e., it is an equidistant time discretization with a uniform time subinterval length of
\Delta t_{j}\equiv\Delta t=T/J
,
\forall j
. This implies that
t_{j}=j\Delta t
for
j=0,1,\ldots,J
. With this, we now define the continuous-time extension of the discrete approximation
\{\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{\Delta t}^{j}\}_{0\leq j\leq J}
by the following piecewise definition:

\mathbf{v}_{\Delta t}(t):=\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{\Delta t}^{j}+\mathbf{f}(t_{j},\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{\Delta t}^{j})(t-t_{j})+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}(t_{j},\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{\Delta t}^{j})(\mathbf{W}(t)-\mathbf{W}(t_{j}))\text{ for }t\in[t_{j},t_{j+1}),

(2.7)

with
j=0,1,\ldots,J-1
, or equivalently as

\mathbf{v}_{\Delta t}(t):=\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{\Delta t}^{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{f}(\eta_{\Delta t}(s),\bar{\mathbf{v}}_{\Delta t}(s))\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}(\eta_{\Delta t}(s),\bar{\mathbf{v}}_{\Delta t}(s))\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}(s),

(2.8)

where
\bar{\mathbf{v}}_{\Delta t}(s)
is defined as the piecewise constant process
\bar{\mathbf{v}}_{\Delta t}(t):=\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{\Delta t}^{j}
, for
t\in[t_{j},t_{j+1})
,
\forall j=0,1,\ldots,J-1
, and likewise
\eta_{\Delta t}(t):=t_{j}=j\Delta t=jT/J
for
t\in[t_{j},t_{j+1})
,
\forall j=0,1,\ldots,J-1
(in this sense we can think of this partition as being tagged by the left end-points
t_{j}
). Note how
\mathbf{v}_{\Delta t}(t)
and
\bar{\mathbf{v}}_{\Delta t}(t)
coincide with the discrete solution at the gridpoints, i.e.,
\mathbf{v}_{\Delta t}(t_{j})=\bar{\mathbf{v}}_{\Delta t}(t_{j})=\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{\Delta t}^{j}
,
\forall j=0,1,\ldots,J
. As discussed, it is then immediate that this process recovers the strong solution to (
2.5
) (i.e., (
2.3
)–(
2.4
)) through the estimate

\mathbb{E}\left[\underset{t\in[0,T]}{\sup}\{\left\|\mathbf{v}(t)-\mathbf{v}_{\Delta t}(t)\right\|_{2}^{2}\}\right]\lesssim O(\Delta t),

(2.9)

for
\Delta t
small. Based then on the previous discussion concerning the consistency, stability, and convergence of the explicit EM scheme defined by (
2.6
)–(
2.8
), and through (
2.9
), we henceforth associate the continuous-time extension of the discrete EM process with the strong solution of the CGNS, as a way to also further clarify the subsequent notation. As such, in the sequel, we have
\mathbf{x}\approx\mathbf{x}_{\Delta t}
and
\mathbf{y}\approx\mathbf{y}_{\Delta t}
, as defined in (
2.7
) or (
2.8
), with this association being true or exact in the limit as
\Delta t\to 0^{+}
(via (
2.9
)). This association is explicitly made through
\mathbf{x}^{j}:=\mathbf{x}(t_{j})\approx\mathbf{x}_{\Delta t}(t_{j})=\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{\Delta t}^{j}
and
\mathbf{y}^{j}:=\mathbf{y}(t_{j})\approx\mathbf{y}_{\Delta t}(t_{j})=\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\Delta t}^{j}
, where generally in this work the superscript
\boldsymbol{\cdot}^{\,j}
denotes the discrete approximation of the continuous form of the respective variable when evaluated on
t_{j}
under this regime, which through (
2.9
) and the continuity of the model parameters in (
2.3
)–(
2.4
) provided by assumption
(3)
and assumption
(9)
, which is outlined in what follows, likewise recovers the continuous-time functional in the limit. For example,
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j}\equiv\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}(t_{j},\mathbf{x}(t_{j}))
, and to make things concrete in terms of the convergence, by using
\mathbf{x}
as an example, we have from (
2.9
) that

\lim_{\Delta t\to 0^{+}}\mathbf{x}_{\Delta t}(t)=\mathbf{x}(t),\ \forall t\in[0,T]\Leftrightarrow\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J^{\prime}\Delta t=T^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}_{\Delta t}^{J^{\prime}}=\mathbf{x}(T^{\prime}),\ \forall T^{\prime}\in[0,T],

where for the remainder of this work the limits being taken as
\Delta t\to 0^{+}
are to be understood in this sense. As such, under this framework, we discretize (
2.3
)–(
2.4
) as (note the form of (
2.6
)):

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{j+1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{x}^{j}+\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{y}^{j}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x},j}\right)\Delta t+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{x},j}\sqrt{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{1}^{j}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{x},j}\sqrt{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{2}^{j},

(2.10)

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}^{j+1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{y}^{j}+\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{y}^{j}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y},j}\right)\Delta t+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{y},j}\sqrt{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{1}^{j}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{y},j}\sqrt{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{2}^{j},

(2.11)

where
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{1}^{j}
and
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{2}^{j}
are mutually independent complex standard Gaussian random noises, and
\Delta t
is assumed to be sufficiently small (or equivalently
J
is sufficiently large).

This notational setup and formulation of the continuous-time extension of the EM discretized solution to the CGNS are required to understand the proofs to the subsequent results presented this work, and which can be found in the appropriate appendices.

Accompanying the conditional Gaussianity provided by Theorem
2.1
, we further assume the following additional conditions (which concur with assumptions
(1)
–
(8)
from before):

\blacktriangleright

Since the focus of this work is to develop a martingale-free framework for the optimal nonlinear posterior state estimation and sampling equations through the EM discretization scheme described by (
2.5
)–(
2.11
), we point to the following works (and references therein), which analyze and prove the uniform or final time instant mean-square or mean-absolute error, and pathwise convergence of the explicit EM method, both in the strong and weak sense, with the strong order of convergence being
p=1/2
, and with the weak one being
p=1

[
138
,
139
,
140
,
141
,
142
,
143
]
. These results are generally established under conditions equivalent to
(1)
–
(8)
assumed here (or weaker), along with the additional assumptions:

\displaystyle\text{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}(9)}}}\quad\left\|\mathbf{f}(t,\mathbf{z},\mathbf{u})-\mathbf{f}(s,\mathbf{z},\mathbf{u})\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}(t,\mathbf{z})-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}(s,\mathbf{z})\right\|_{2}\leq L_{6}\big(1+(\left\|\mathbf{z}\right\|^{2}_{2}+\left\|\mathbf{u}\right\|^{2}_{2})^{1/2}\big)|t-s|^{1/2},

\displaystyle\text{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}(10)}}}\quad\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{x}(0)-\mathbf{x}_{\Delta t}(0)\right\|^{2}_{2}+\left\|\mathbf{y}(0)-\mathbf{y}_{\Delta t}(0)\right\|^{2}_{2}\right]\leq L_{7}\Delta t,

where
\mathbf{z}\in C^{0}([0,T];\mathbb{C}^{k})
,
\mathbf{u}\in C^{0}([0,T];\mathbb{C}^{l})
, and
\mathbf{f}:=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}_{k\times k}&\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{l\times k}&\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{x}\\
\mathbf{y}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x}}\\
\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y}}\end{pmatrix}
,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}:=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{x}}&\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{x}}\\
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{y}}&\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{y}}\end{pmatrix}
, for
L_{6},L_{7}>0
, with
\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{x}_{\Delta t}(t)\\
\mathbf{y}_{\Delta t}(t)\end{pmatrix}
being the continuous-time extension of the EM discretization corresponding to (
2.3
)–(
2.4
) (see (
2.7
)–(
2.8
)). Especially, and most importantly, under the optimality in the mean-square sense that the posterior statistics (filter or smoother) enjoy, the expected uniform (in
[0,T]
) squared error of the EM method (which is stronger to to the uniform or terminal time expected absolute error) is bounded by
O(\Delta t)

[
133
,
144
]
, where
\Delta t
is the norm or mesh of the temporal partition of
[0,T]
, and under assumptions
(1)
–
(10)
, the strong solution of the CGNS provided by
(1)
–
(4)
is exactly recovered in the limit as
\Delta t\to 0^{+}

[
145
,
146
]
. This has the important implication that martingale theory can be avoided in proving most results in the continuous-time CGNS framework, since the desired results can be instead established for the discrete-time case using such time discretization schemes, for which then the pertinent results for the case of continuous time can be obtained from these by a formal passage to the limit as the maximal discretization time step vanishes (or the number of time subintervals grows unboundedly)
[
100
]
. This provides a unified treatment of the discrete- and continuous-time settings of CGNSs. This is further made possible exactly because the optimal nonlinear posterior state estimation equations, and associated sampling SDEs, are defined by first-order differential operators (infinitesimal generators), which by strong and weak consistency guarantee a first-order coherence between the discrete- and continuous-time counterparts of these dynamical equations.

(11)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{ij}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{x})|^{4}+|\mathbf{f}_{i}^{\mathbf{y}}(t,\mathbf{x})|^{4}+|\Sigma^{\mathbf{y},1}_{ij}(t,\mathbf{x})|^{4}+|\Sigma^{\mathbf{y},2}_{ij}(t,\mathbf{x})|^{4}\right]\mathrm{d}t<+\infty
. The assumption on the finiteness of the quantity
\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[|(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{\mathbf{y}}(t,\mathbf{x}))|^{4}\right]\mathrm{d}t
is also essential in the stochastic optimal control problem based on (
2.4
), as it restricts the additive controls to the class of expected
L^{4}
functionals, i.e., which have their expectation in the Bochner space
L^{4}([0,T];\mathbb{C}^{p})
(see Chapter 3 of Kolodziej
[
126
]
and Kolodziej & Mohler
[
136
]
)

(12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{y}(0)\right\|_{2}^{4}\right]<+\infty
. This also aids in providing the control
\mathbb{E}\left[\underset{t\in[0,T]}{\sup}\{\left\|\mathbf{y}(t)\right\|_{2}^{4}\}\right]<+\infty

[
126
,
147
]
.

\blacktriangleright

Like before, the sufficient assumptions
(11)
and
(12)
can be slightly weakened
[
126
,
136
]
.

With these, we can then yield the optimal nonlinear filter state estimation equations as showcased in the following theorem. In what follows, the subscript ”
f
” is used to denote the filter conditional Gaussian statistics, which appropriately stands for filter. The filter conditional Gaussian statistics are also known as the filter posterior mean and filter posterior covariance under the Bayesian inference dynamics framework.

Theorem 2.2

(
Optimal Nonlinear Filter State Estimation Equations
)
.

Let the assumptions of Theorem
2.1
and the additional regularity conditions
(9)
–
(12)
, to hold. Then for any
t\in[0,T]
the
\mathcal{F}^{\mathbf{x}}_{t}
-measurable Gaussian statistics of the Gaussian conditional distribution

\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}(t)\big|\mathbf{x}(s),s\leq t\big)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}_{l}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}(t),\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(t)),

where

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}(t):=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{y}(t)\big|\mathbf{x}(s),s\leq t\right]\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(t):=\mathbb{E}\left[(\mathbf{y}(t)-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}(t))(\mathbf{y}(t)-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}(t))^{\dagger}\big|\mathbf{x}(s),s\leq t\right],

are the unique continuous solutions of the system of optimal nonlinear filter equations,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}(t)

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y}})\mathrm{d}t+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}})^{\dagger})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}-(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x}})\mathrm{d}t),

(2.12)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(t)

\displaystyle=\big(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y}}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y}})^{\dagger}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}-(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}})^{\dagger})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}})\big)\mathrm{d}t,

(2.13)

with initial values
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}(0)=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{y}(0)\big|\mathbf{x}(0)\right]
and
\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(0)=\mathbb{E}\left[(\mathbf{y}(0)-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}(0))(\mathbf{y}(0)-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}(0))^{\dagger}\big|\mathbf{x}(0)\right]
, where the noise interactions through the Gramians (with respect to rows) are defined as

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})(t,\mathbf{x}):=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{x})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{x})^{\dagger}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{x})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{x})^{\dagger},

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})(t,\mathbf{x}):=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{y}}(t,\mathbf{x})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{y}}(t,\mathbf{x})^{\dagger}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{y}}(t,\mathbf{x})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{y}}(t,\mathbf{x})^{\dagger},

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})(t,\mathbf{x}):=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{x})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{y}}(t,\mathbf{x})^{\dagger}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{x})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{y}}(t,\mathbf{x})^{\dagger},\quad(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})(t,\mathbf{x}):=(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})(t,\mathbf{x})^{\dagger}.

Furthermore, if the initial covariance matrix
\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(0)
is positive-definite (
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.), then all the matrices
\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(t)
, for
t\in[0,T]
, remain positive-definite (
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.).

See Appendix
6.2
for the proof. We highlight that Theorem
2.2
can also be established under sufficient assumptions that only guarantee the existence of a weak, i.e., martingale, solution to (
2.3
)–(
2.4
), meaning the regularity conditions in
(1)
–
(12)
can be substantially relaxed
[
126
]
.

The filter mean equation under the Bayesian inference framework for DA has an intuitive structure. The first two terms on the right-hand side of (
2.12
), specifically
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y}}
, represent the forecast mean derived from the latent dynamics in (
2.4
). The remaining terms account for the correction of the posterior mean state based on partial observations. The matrix-valued factor preceding the innovation or measurement pre-fit residual
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}(t)-(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x}})\mathrm{d}t
resembles the Kalman gain in classical Kalman filter theory, expressed as
\mathbf{K}=(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}})^{\dagger})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}
, which quantifies the influence of observations on updating the predicted state. Even if the latent dynamics do not explicitly depend on
\mathbf{x}
, the observational process in (
2.3
) couples the observed and latent components, allowing observations to adjust the state estimation and correct model forecasts. Additionally,
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}(t)
satisfies a random ordinary differential equation linear in
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}(t)

[
148
,
149
,
150
,
151
]
, while
\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(t)
is the solution to a random Riccati equation
[
152
,
153
,
154
]
, with coefficients depending on the observable random vector
\mathbf{x}
.

The CGNS framework also provides closed analytic formulae for optimal smoother state estimation. The smoother posterior distribution at time
t\in[0,T]
leverages observational information from the entire interval, resulting in a more accurate, less uncertain, and unbiased estimated state compared to the filter solution. To solve the optimal nonlinear smoother equations, a forward pass (filtering) from
t=0
to
t=T
is followed by a backward pass (smoothing) from
t=T
to
t=0
. The forthcoming theorem presents the optimal nonlinear smoother equations, using the subscript
s
(note the lack of italicization) to denote smoother conditional Gaussian statistics, which should not be confused with the time variable

s
in
\mathbf{x}(s)
or
\mathbf{x}^{s}
. These smoother conditional Gaussian statistics are referred to as the smoother posterior mean and covariance within the Bayesian inference framework. But, for the assertion of this result to be feasible for the case of our martingale-free framework, we enforce a final extraneous assumption on the model parameters of the CGNS, specifically the noise feedbacks of the hidden process:

(13)

Adopting the temporal discretization setup from the beginning of the section, we assume that

a_{\Delta t}(M):=\underset{\theta>0}{\inf}\Bigg\{\mathbb{E}\Bigg[e^{\theta\big(M\big(1+\underset{0\leq j\leq J}{\max}\big\{\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|_{2}^{2}+\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|_{2}^{2}\big\}\big)-1/\Delta t\big)}\Bigg]\Bigg\}=o(\Delta t),

as
\Delta t\to 0^{+}
, for every
M=M(\omega)
with
\mathbb{P}(M<+\infty)=1
.

\blacktriangleright

As illuminated by the proof framework in this work, this is admittedly a rather ad hoc condition. This restriction essentially enforces a temporal control onto the uncertainty levels that should be expected in the latent phase space from a free unconditional run of the CGNS, without being too restrictive (e.g., assuming constant feedbacks). As an example, this condition is trivially satisfied if
\underset{t\in[0,T]}{\sup}\big\{\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}_{m}(t,\mathbf{x})\big\|_{2}^{2}\big\}<+\infty
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s., for
m=1,2
, which is in many applications a natural condition being satisfied; for example this holds when the noise feedbacks are continuous in time for almost every path of
\mathbf{x}
with
T<+\infty
. The use of this assumption will be in developing a consistent optimal discrete-time smoother, and as is showcased in Appendix
6.3
this assumption can be slightly weakened or altered while still being conformant to our proof framework under dominated convergence results. In such a scenario, this assumption can be regarded as a “Courant-Friedrichs-Lewy-type” convergence restriction enforced onto the time step
\Delta t
from an asymptotic expansion-based standpoint. Specifically, by requiring
\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[e^{\theta\big(M\big(1+\underset{0\leq j\leq J}{\max}\big\{\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|_{2}^{2}+\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|_{2}^{2}\big\}\big)-1/\Delta t\big)}\Bigg]<0
for
\theta>0
, we get
\displaystyle\Delta t<\frac{1}{M\big(1+\underset{0\leq j\leq J}{\max}\big\{\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|_{2}^{2}+\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|_{2}^{2}\big\}\big)}

\mathbb{P}
-a.s, where due to the explicit nature of our time discretization scheme (by the EM method), then the right-hand side of this inequality can be regarded as a ratio of the ”length-scale” to the ”velocity” of the uncertainty levels over the latent state space (see expressions of
M
in the proof of Lemma
6.5
).

To establish the assertion of this theorem we require various Lemmas, which we state and prove, along with the proof of this theorem, in Appendix
6.3
.

Theorem 2.3

(
Optimal Nonlinear Smoother State Estimation Backward Equations
)
.

Let the assumptions of Theorem
2.1
and the additional regularity conditions
(9)
–
(13)
, to hold. In addition, let

\mathbb{P}\left(\underset{t\in[0,T]}{\mathrm{inf}}\{\mathrm{det}(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(t))\}>0\right)=1,

(2.14)

since it is not guaranteed that the positive-definiteness of the filter posterior covariance tensor is preserved in the backward pass. Then the system of optimal nonlinear smoother equations is given by the following backward differential equations,

\displaystyle\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}}(t)

\displaystyle=-(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y}}-\mathbf{B}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}))\mathrm{d}t+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}(\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x}})\mathrm{d}t),

(2.15)

\displaystyle\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{s}}}}(t)

\displaystyle=-\big((\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{B}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1})\mathbf{R}_{\text{{s}}}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{s}}}(\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{B}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1})^{\dagger}-\mathbf{B}\big)\mathrm{d}t,

(2.16)

for
T\geq t\geq 0
, with
t
running backward, where the auxiliary matrices
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
are defined in (
6.14
)–(
6.15
). The backward-arrow notation on the left-hand side of (
2.15
)–(
2.16
) and in
\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}
on right-hand side of (
2.15
) is to be understood as

\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}}(t):=\lim_{\Delta t\to 0}\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}(t)-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}(t+\Delta t)\right),\quad\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{s}}}}(t):=\lim_{\Delta t\to 0}\left(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{s}}}(t)-\mathbf{R}_{\text{{s}}}(t+\Delta t)\right),\quad\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}(t):=\lim_{\Delta t\to 0}\left(\mathbf{x}(t)-\mathbf{x}(t+\Delta t)\right),

for
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}(t)
and
\mathbf{R}_{\text{{s}}}(t)
being the mean and covariance of the smoother posterior distribution at time
t
which is Gaussian,
\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}(t)\big|\mathbf{x}(s),s\in[0,T]\big)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}_{l}\big(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}(t),\mathbf{R}_{\text{{s}}}(t)\big)
. In other words, the notation
\overleftarrow{\frac{\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\cdot}}{\mathrm{d}t}}
corresponds to the negative of the usual derivative, which means the system in (
2.15
)–(
2.16
) is meant to be solved backward over
[0,T]
. The ”starting” values for the smoother posterior statistics,
\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}(T),\mathbf{R}_{\text{{s}}}(T)\right)
, are the same as those of the corresponding filter estimates at the endpoint
t=T
,
\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}(T),\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(T)\right)
.

We would like to note here that the smoother evolution equations in (
2.15
)–(
2.16
) are consistent with representations through Markov smoothing semigroups associated with the transport equation for the smoother conditional distribution corresponding to similar nonlinear partially observed backward diffusion flow models
[
155
]
.

3
Optimal Conditional Sampling Procedures

The posterior distribution from filtering or smoothing provides optimal pointwise state estimation in the mean-square sense, where ”pointwise” refers to estimation at a fixed time instant. However, in many applications, it is crucial to obtain sampled trajectories of the hidden processes conditioned on observations. These sampled trajectories reflect temporal statistics that cannot be accessed through pointwise posterior estimates alone. Unfortunately, relying solely on posterior distributions is insufficient to generate unbiased, dynamically consistent trajectories. This is because each point in a sampled trajectory must account for the nonlinear temporal dependence and correlations with neighboring points. In practice, the posterior mean time series is often used as a surrogate for the hidden variable, but this approach overlooks the temporal correlation contained in the posterior covariance, namely the correlated uncertainty. It leads to the failure of capturing many key dynamical features, such as the autocorrelation function (ACF). Simply drawing independent samples from the posterior distributions at different time points results in a noisy time series that misses underlying dynamics due to the lack of temporal correlation integration.

The closed-form analytic formulae presented in this section provide an efficient and dynamically consistent method for sampling unobserved trajectories of
\mathbf{y}
conditioned on the observed
\mathbf{x}
. This applies to both forward sampling (up to the current time) and backward sampling (over the entire observation period). These sampling methods allow to improve the understanding of nonlinear dynamical features in CTNDSs. All subsequent results assume the validity of assumptions
(1)
–
(13)
(or their weaker counterparts), along with any additional assumptions outlined in Section
2.4
.

3.1
Filter-Based Forward Sampling

In the main theorem of this subsection, we state the formula for optimal nonlinear forward sampling of the unobserved process in a CGNS, conditioned on observable data up to the current time. Its proof can be found in Appendix
6.4
. In what follows, the subscript ”
f
” stands for ”filter” since the forward sampling procedure is based on the filter solution; see also Theorem
3.2
for a consistency result.

Theorem 3.1

(
Optimal Nonlinear Forward Sampling Formula
)
.

When conditioned on the natural filtration of
\mathcal{F}
w.r.t.
\{\mathbf{x}(s)\}_{s\leq t}
and
\mathbf{y}(t)
,
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}(t)}:=\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\mathbf{x}}\vee\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\mathbf{y}(t)}=\sigma\big(\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\mathbf{x}}\cup\sigma(\mathbf{y}(t))\big)
(also known as the join of the (sub-)
\sigma
-algebras), for some
t\in[0,T]
, then the optimal (in the mean-square sense) strategy for sampling the trajectories associated with the unobservable variables
\mathbf{y}
at time
t
, which we denote by
\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{f}}}
, is given by the following SDE:

\mathrm{d}\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{f}}}(t)=\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}+(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma})(\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{f}}}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}})\mathrm{d}t+(\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}})^{1/2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_{\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{f}}}},

(3.1)

where
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_{\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{f}}}}
is an
l
-dimensional complex-valued white noise source that is mutually independent from
\mathbf{W}_{1}
and
\mathbf{W}_{2}
,
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
are given as in (
6.14
)–(
6.15
), and
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
is defined as

\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(t,\mathbf{x}):=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{x})^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}(t,\mathbf{x})\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{x}).

(3.2)

The
\boldsymbol{\cdot}^{1/2}
denotes the square root of a matrix. It also holds that
\mathbf{B}(t,\mathbf{x})+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(t)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(t,\mathbf{x})\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(t)
is necessarily
nonnegative-definite
\mathbb{P}
-a.s., or that

\mathbb{P}\left(\underset{t\in[0,T]}{\mathrm{inf}}\{\mathrm{det}(\mathbf{B}(t,\mathbf{x})+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(t)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(t,\mathbf{x})\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(t))\}>0\right)=1,

(3.3)

and so its square root is unique as can be shown by its eigendecomposition.

Similar to the filtering procedure in Theorem
2.2
, the forward sampling procedure in Theorem
3.1
is a sequential method, which makes use of the information only in the past to compute the pathwise value at the current time instant, thus allowing it to be integrated into online schemes where the observations arrive in a serial manner. Furthermore, the formula given in (
3.1
) states that the sampled trajectory of
\mathbf{y}
,
\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{f}}}
, is based on the posterior mean estimate of the filter
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}
, but it also includes temporal correlated uncertainties that depend both on the observations and the underlying nonlinear model, both in the deterministic and stochastic dynamics. Actually, through an equivalent representation of the filter posterior mean and covariance matrix (using elementary algebraic manipulations)
[
69
]
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}(t)&=\big((\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y}}\big)\mathrm{d}t+\big(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}\right)^{\dagger}\big)(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}\big(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x}}\mathrm{d}t\big),\\
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(t)&=\big((\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma})^{\dagger}+\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\big)\mathrm{d}t,\end{split}

(3.4)

it becomes quickly apparent that for the exponential mean-square stability of the filter state estimation equations to be satisfied, it is necessary that the real parts of all the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
are negative
[
149
,
150
,
156
,
157
,
158
,
151
,
154
]
, and as such the samples
\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{f}}}
have the tendency to collapse towards the filter mean state
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}
; this convergence assertion is made concrete in Theorem
3.2
and its proof. Notice how this also illustrates the fact that the damping in the filter posterior mean and the forward-based sampled trajectories is the same. For more on the stochastic stability of the optimal nonlinear filter, we recommend the surveys of Chigansky
[
159
]
and van Handel
[
160
]
, and their references therein. As for the stability of the nonlinear filter covariance matrix, see Wang & Lei for the discrete-time case
[
152
]
and Bishop & Del Moral for the continuous-time case
[
154
]
.

Another important observation regarding (
3.1
), is that its noise feedback is not simply
(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{1/2}
as in the original latent dynamical equation (
2.4
), but instead

(\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}})^{1/2}=\left((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})-\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})-\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\big)\right)^{1/2}.

Now, while it is tempting here to presume that this equation has much larger damping in its uncertainty compared to the original model, by enforcing that
\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\preceq(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})
, where ”
\preceq
” is understood in the Loewner partial ordering sense over the closed pointed convex cone of nonnegative-definite matrices, it could very well be the case that this is not actually true. Indeed, observe that for
\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\preceq(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})
to hold, it is equivalent to have

{(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})-\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\succeq\mathbf{0}_{l\times l}}.

Now, while
(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})
is positive-definite by assumption
(5)
, and so is
\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}
for the same reason together with the positive-definiteness of the filter covariance matrix, after possibly the additional assumption of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}
and
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}
being
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. full rank, a simple application of the Lidskii-Mirsky-Wielandt majorization theorem yields
[
161
,
162
]

\mu_{i}((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})-\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}})\leq\rho((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}))-\mu_{i}(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}),

(3.5)

for any
i=1,\ldots,l
(where here we use the notation from Lemma
6.5
). Even under our set of regularity conditions, this leaves enough leeway such that
\mu_{i_{o}}((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})-\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}})<0
for some
i_{o}\in\{1,\ldots,l\}
and appropriately chosen model parameters in (
2.3
)–(
2.4
), which means
\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\not\preceq(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})
. Actually, such an example is chosen in our numerical case study in Section
4
as to illustrate this fact. Intuitively, it means some observations can actually deteriorate the filter posterior state estimation, thus potentially leading to an increase in the uncertainty in the sampled trajectories.

On the other hand, if sufficient conditions are enforced such that
\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\preceq(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})
holds
\mathbb{P}
-a.s., then the uncertainty represented by this noise coefficient takes into account the information from the observations effectively, which is embodied in
\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}
,
(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}
, and
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}
, thus leading to a much smaller overall uncertainty instead. Therefore, the uncertainty, represented by the equilibrium variance, is lower in the forward samples
\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{f}}}
conditioned on the observations, (
3.1
), than the
\mathbf{y}
resulting from a free run of the coupled model, (
2.4
). Of course, this reduction in the uncertainty stems from the information that is provided by the observational time series.

Another useful conclusion that can be drawn from (
3.1
