) stems from looking at the associated forward equation for the residual. Specifically, we define the residual of the sampled trajectory
\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{f}}}
with respect to the filter mean state
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}
, i.e.,
\tilde{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\text{f}}=\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{f}}}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}
; notice how this is different from the mean-fluctuation decomposition in (
6.1
), which is why we do not use the overline notation but instead a tilde (see also Theorem
3.2
). Then, by (
3.1
),
\tilde{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\text{f}}
satisfies the SDE:

\mathrm{d}\tilde{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\text{f}}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\big(\mathrm{d}\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{f}}}+(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\tilde{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\text{f}}\mathrm{d}t+(\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}})^{1/2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_{\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{f}}}}\big).

(3.6)

This equation generates the residual time series, reflecting the uncertainty in the sampled trajectory, which exhibits non-trivial temporal dependence. The temporal behavior of
\tilde{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\text{f}}
in (
3.6
) differs significantly from that of
\mathbf{y}
in (
2.4
), as seen even in the one-dimensional case. Assume
k=l=1
, so both
\mathbf{x}=x
and
\mathbf{y}=y
are one-dimensional, with constant coefficients, where
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}=\Lambda^{x}>0
and
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y}}=\Lambda^{y}<0
. Here,
\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}=R_{f}
converges to a positive constant
R_{\infty}>0
over time, as shown by (
2.13
)
[
163
,
134
]
. The decorrelation time of the marginal, conditional, and joint distributions (since the constant-coefficient CGNS has reversible conditional Gaussianity) equals the reciprocal of its damping coefficient
[
164
]
, per Doob’s theorem
[
165
,
166
,
167
]
, which states that the autocorrelation of a stationary Gaussian Markov process is proportional to
e^{-as}
, where
a
is the damping coefficient. For
\mathbf{A}=A
and
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}=\Gamma
, the decorrelation times for the original system (
2.4
) and the residual of the sampled trajectory (
3.6
) are
-1/\Lambda^{y}>0
and
-1/(A-R_{\infty}\Gamma)>0
, respectively, with the latter holding asymptotically. Then, by assuming the noise feedbacks of the system are positive, we have that
-(A-R_{\infty}\Gamma)>-\Lambda^{y}>0
, which shows that the decorrelation time of the residual is shorter due to the added information from observations. This means that, in a linear time-invariant CGNS, the residual decorrelates faster than the unobserved process. However, this does not necessarily imply lower uncertainty in the forward samples.

We conclude this subsection with the following theorem, which links the pointwise Gaussian statistics of the forward-sampled trajectories to those of the filter posterior distribution. This establishes that the pointwise Gaussian statistics of the trajectories generated by the forward sampling procedure are consistent, at each time instant, with those of the filtering posterior distribution. See Appendix
6.5
for the proof.

Theorem 3.2

(
Consistency Between Forward Sampling and Filter State Estimation
)
.

Assume that the real parts of all the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
are negative (
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.). Then, the conditional Gaussian nonlinear filter estimate, (
2.12
)–(
2.13
), can be recovered by applying a mean-fluctuation decomposition to the forward sampling equation (
3.1
). In other words:

(a)

The ensemble average of the forward sampling equation (
3.1
), conditioned on the natural filtration of
\mathcal{F}
w.r.t.
\{\mathbf{x}(s)\}_{s\leq t}
and
\mathbf{y}(t)
,
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}(t)}
, for some
t\in[0,T]
, is the time evolution of the posterior mean in (
2.12
).

(b)

The ensemble average of the quadratic form of the residual equation of (
3.1
), conditioned on the natural filtration of
\mathcal{F}
w.r.t.
\{\mathbf{x}(s)\}_{s\leq t}
and
\mathbf{y}(t)
,
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}(t)}
, for some
t\in[0,T]
, is the time evolution of the posterior covariance in (
2.13
).

3.2
Smoother-Based Backward Sampling

The forward sampling formula in Theorem
3.1
is derived from the nonlinear filter estimate, as outlined in Theorem
3.2
. Since smoothing provides a more accurate and less uncertain state estimation, it is natural to consider a corresponding smoother-based sampling formula for the latent variables. This formula runs backward in time and is conditioned on the full sequence of measurements from the observable variables. The following result explicitly presents this backward sampling procedure for the latent variables, where its proof can be found in Appendix
6.6
. As before, the subscript ”
s
’’ denotes ”smoother’’, indicating that this sampler is based on the backward smoother solution. See also Theorem
3.4
for the consistency result.

Theorem 3.3

(
Optimal Nonlinear Backward Sampling Formula
)
.

When conditioned on the terminating observable
\sigma
-algebra,
\mathcal{F}_{T}^{\mathbf{x}}
, i.e., a complete realization of the observed variables
\mathbf{x}
, then the optimal (in the mean-square sense) strategy for sampling the trajectories associated with the unobservable variables
\mathbf{y}
at time
T\geq t\geq 0
, which we denote by
\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}
, satisfies the following explicit formula:

\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}}(t)=(-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}-\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y}}+(\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{B}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1})(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}-\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}))\mathrm{d}t+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}\big(\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x}})\mathrm{d}t\big)+\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_{\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}},

(3.7)

for
T\geq t\geq 0
and where
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_{\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}}
are independent
forward
white-noise increments in
reverse
time,
\tau=T-t
, and
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}
are given in (
6.14
)–(
6.15
). As shown in Theorem
2.2
, the square root of
\mathbf{B}
exists and is unique (
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.). The backward-arrow notation on the left-hand is to be understood as

\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}}(t):=\lim_{\Delta t\to 0}\left(\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}(t)-\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}(t+\Delta t)\right),

and likewise for
\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}
on the right-hand side, where on the right-hand side of (
3.7
),
\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}
is evaluated at
t+\Delta t
while all other coefficients and the filter estimate are being calculated at
t
. The formula in (
3.7
) starts from
t=T
and it is ran backwards towards
t=0
. The initial value of
\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}
in (
3.7
) is drawn from the conditional Gaussian distribution corresponding to the filter estimate at
t=T
,
\mathcal{N}_{l}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}(T),\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(T))
, due to the fact that the filter and the smoother estimates coincide at the end point.

Comparing (
3.7
) with the true underlying dynamics of
\mathbf{y}
in (
2.4
), beyond the usage of the filter mean for the deterministic dynamics, the backward sampling equation involves the extra forcing term

(\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{B}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1})(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}-\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}})\mathrm{d}t+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}\big(\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x}})\mathrm{d}t\big).

This correction term is similar to the one in the optimal nonlinear smoother mean equation, (
2.15
):

\mathbf{B}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}})\mathrm{d}t+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}\big(\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x}})\mathrm{d}t\big),

which takes into account the information provided by the observations and builds a connection between the smoother solution and backward sampled trajectories. Specifically, for the case of the backward samples, this correction term plays the important role of forcing and driving the sampled trajectory to meander around the filter mean state
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}
, under some suitable assumptions on the model parameters appearing in
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
. However, due to the memory of the latent process, the system response of the forcing has a delayed effect, and actually fluctuates around the smoother mean
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}
, which can be seen by the following corollary to Theorem
3.3
, which provides an alternative way to express the backward sampling formula is as,

\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\hat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\text{s}}(t)=\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}}-(\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{B}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1})(\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}})\mathrm{d}t+\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_{\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}}.

(3.8)

To see why this holds, we simply take the difference between (
3.7
) and (
2.15
) and perform the necessary algebra. The result in (
3.8
) presents the backward sampling framework in a form analogous to the forward sampling strategy in (
3.1
), showing that the backward-sampled trajectory oscillates around the smoother mean state. Furthermore, note that the deterministic component of the forcing has feedback of the form
\mathbf{B}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}+\mathbf{A}
, which by (
2.16
),

\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}}(t)=-(\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{B}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}-(\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y}}-\mathbf{B}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}})\mathrm{d}t+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}(\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x}}\mathrm{d}t),

from (
2.15
), and the theory of random ODEs, governs the stability of the smoother covariance through its spectrum’s projection onto the real axis, which must remain positive (accounting for the minus signs in (
2.15
)–(
2.16
))
[
158
,
168
,
169
]
. To simplify the role of observations, consider no noise cross-interaction and constant noise strength in
\mathbf{y}
, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}
is constant. In this case, by (
2.13
),
\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}
positively correlates with the observational noise
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}
, with a lower uncertainty in
\mathbf{x}
leading to a smaller
\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}
(in the trace sense) and a larger correction weight (since the quadratic term in its random Riccati is being weighted by
-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}

[
154
]
). It means observations play an important role in determining the backward sampled trajectories. Concerning the dynamical properties of the CGNS, the backward sampling formula also possesses the enticing feature that it retains the structure of the latent dynamics, which has the important implication that the temporal ACF, as well as other higher-order moments and temporal correlations, and even the marginal of both components of the state space and joint PDFs, can be accurately recovered using these sampled trajectories. An example towards this direction is showcased in the numerical case study included in Section
4
.

Analogous now to Theorem
3.2
, the following result establishes a consistency connection between the pointwise statistics of the backward sampled trajectories and the Gaussian statistics of the smoother posterior distribution. The proof to this result can be found in Appendix
6.7

Theorem 3.4

(
Consistency Between Backward Sampling and Smoother State Estimation
)
.

Assume that the real parts of all the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{B}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}
are positive (
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.). Additionally, assume that at
t=T
the backward samples coincide with the conditional Gaussian nonlinear smoother mean. Then, the conditional Gaussian nonlinear smoother estimate, (
2.15
)–(
2.16
), can be recovered by applying a mean-fluctuation decomposition to the backward sampling equation (
3.7
). In other words:

(a)

The ensemble average of the backward sampling equation (
3.7
), conditioned on the natural filtration of
\mathcal{F}
w.r.t.
\{\mathbf{x}(s)\}_{s\leq T}
,
\mathcal{F}_{T}^{\mathbf{x}}
, i.e., a complete realization of the observed variables
\mathbf{x}
, is the time evolution of the smoother mean in (
2.15
).

(b)

The ensemble average of the quadratic form of the residual equation of (
3.7
), conditioned on the natural filtration of
\mathcal{F}
w.r.t.
\{\mathbf{x}(s)\}_{s\leq T}
,
\mathcal{F}_{T}^{\mathbf{x}}
, i.e., a complete realization of the observed variables
\mathbf{x}
, is the time evolution of the smoother covariance in (
2.16
).

3.3
A Hierarchy of Uncertainty in the Sampling Formulae for the Unobserved Variables

Based on the results provided by Theorems
3.1
–
3.4
, as well as the discussions accompanying these conclusions in Section
3.1
and
3.2
, respectively, it is then possible, under some suitable assumptions, to infer the emergence of a hierarchy in the noise feedback components of the sampling uncertainty, which can be expressed through a double matrix inequality between the noise feedback matrix in the original equation driving the latent dynamics, (
2.4
), and the noise amplitude matrices appearing in the optimal nonlinear forward and backward sampling formulas, (
3.1
) and (
3.7
), respectively. We state this observation in the following remark.

Remark 3.5

(
Hierarchy in the Uncertainty of the Latent Dynamics
)
.

Let ”
\succeq
” to define the Loewner partial order over the closed and pointed convex cone of nonnegative-definite matrices in the space of self-adjoint matrices, with its interior being the open blunt convex cone of positive-definite matrices (in the spectral operator norm). This means
\mathbf{R}\succeq\mathbf{T}
if
\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{T}
is nonnegative definite, and
\mathbf{R}\succ\mathbf{T}
if
\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{T}
is positive definite. Then, it always holds that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\succeq\mathbf{B}
, with strict inequality if
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}
is
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. of full rank, and that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.
\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\succeq\mathbf{B}
, with strict inequality if
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}
is
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. of full rank (with the latter also being important in establishing the stochastic controllability of the CGNS). These are true for any
t\in[0,T]
.

As for,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\succeq\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}
, or, equivalently,
(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})\succeq\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}
, it does not necessarily always hold even under the current set of assumptions
(1)
–
(13)
, including the additional ones considered in Sections
2
and
3
thus far, as already discussed in Section
3.1
(see (
3.5
)). Following an analysis similar to the one in Lemma
6.5
, specifically through the Laplace transform method for bounds in expectation of extremal eigenvalues
[
170
]
, we have that such a result can hold in expectation (or in the weak-sense), i.e.,
\mathbb{E}\left[(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})\right]\succeq\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\right]
, with
\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\cdot}\right]
denoting the full expectation, if sufficient conditions are assumed to provide the control

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{tr}\left(e^{\theta\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})-\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\big)}\right)\right]\leq 1,\ \forall\theta<0,

As such, we have:

\begin{gathered}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\succeq\mathbf{B},\quad\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\succeq\mathbf{B},\ \forall t\in[0,T]\ (\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.}),\\
\forall t\in[0,T]:\ \mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{tr}\left(e^{\theta\big(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}-\mathbf{B}-\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\big)}\right)\right]\leq 1,\ \forall\theta<0\Leftrightarrow(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})\succeq_{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}.\end{gathered}

(3.9)

Essentially, Remark
3.5
asserts that an increasing incorporation of information in the simulation of the latent dynamics leads to a decrease in their sample-to-sample fluctuations, since
(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})
,
\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}
, and
\mathbf{B}
are the noise feedbacks matrices corresponding to (
2.4
), (
3.1
), and (
3.7
), respectively. The result in (
3.9
) does so via a concise, intuitive explanation. When not conditioning on any of the observations, the fluctuations component of the latent uncertainty is large. However, by incorporating the information provided by the observed variables, this uncertainty can potentially decrease. If we move forward, i.e., by conditioning on only up to the current observational values, the initial latent uncertainty defined by the sum of the Gramians of the noise feedback matrices in the unobservable process,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}
, is ”corrected” by the matrix
(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})-\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}
to give the forward-based sampling noise amplitude
\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}
. As noted by the discussion following the proof of Theorem
3.1
, specifically (
3.5
), it does not always hold that
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\succeq\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}
, but assuming the validity of (
3.9
), then this matrix inequality (in either the strong or weak sense) conveys that the information gained by incorporating the observations of
\mathbf{x}
into the state estimation, is inversely proportional to the observational process’ uncertainty weighted by the noise cross-interaction, i.e.,
(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})
, but which is then counterbalanced at the same time by the information that is lost due to the inherent uncertainty of not knowing the unobservable process
\mathbf{y}
,
-\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}
, with this term being the weighted factor (weighted by the filter posterior covariance) which defines the quadratic dynamics in the filter covariance’s random evolution equation. Going further than that, when moving backwards, i.e., by conditioning on a complete batch of observations and then proceeding with smoothing, always guarantees a decrease in the overall, as well as component-wise, uncertainty of the hidden process, with the noise feedback now decreasing down to
\mathbf{B}
, with the difference in fluctuations being just the (generally) nonnegative-definite matrix
(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})
, since no filter uncertainty is adopted, and so the only uncertainty stems from that of the sampled trajectory of
\mathbf{x}
,
(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}
, and any potential noise cross-interactions that emerge by the model structure, i.e., whether
(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})\equiv\mathbf{0}_{k\times l}
or not; this further showcases, analytically, the unbiasedness of the smoother estimate.

It should be noted that we do not make any explicit assertions about the overall uncertainty of the system and, by extension, that of the posterior estimated states. We are just presenting some results concerning its constituents, mainly in this case the noise component of the dynamical uncertainty. As can be clearly seen from the forward and backward fluctuation sampling equations, (
6.64
) and (
6.67
), the uncertainty is partly controlled by the noise feedback matrices appearing in (
3.9
), but also by the corresponding damping multiplicative factors which help relax the respective sample state towards the corresponding posterior mean in an exponentially fast fashion, or, equivalently, by forcing the magnitude of the fluctuations down to zero (where these damping terms, as already noted, also control the mean-square stability of the associated posterior statistics through the projection of their spectrum onto the abscissa of the complex plane
[
149
,
150
,
156
,
157
,
158
,
168
,
151
,
169
]
). So, while the noise amplitude matrices satisfy a hierarchy such as the one indicated in Remark
3.5
, this only partly illuminates the temporal behavior of the uncertainty of the system and how the observations affect it. After all, it is known that the filter and smoother estimated states will always decrease the overall state uncertainty regardless of how informative (or not) the current measurement or observation is. As such, even if the filter noise amplitude might be larger, there is always a trade-off between damping and noise, as indicated by the bias-variance decomposition of the mean-square error
[
171
]
, which should necessarily lead to a less uncertain state by the optimality in this metric. See Section
4.3
and Figure
4.3
for further discussion on the matter.

3.4
Pathwise Error Metrics for the Posterior Mean Time Series and the Sampled Trajectories

In many applications, the skill in the probabilistic estimated state is quantified by the pathwise error between the posterior mean time series and the truth. The motivation of adopting such a pathwise approach is that for a Gaussian posterior distribution, the posterior mean estimate at each fixed time instant is a convex combination of the prior mean at that time instant and the pointwise maximum likelihood estimate, a feature that is inherited from the conjugacy of the model and its inclusion in the exponential family of distributions
[
172
]
.

The pathwise error between the posterior mean time series and the truth is easy to compute in practice, by just taking the difference between the two with respect to some norm at each time instant, which forms an error time series. However, by computing the pathwise error solely via the posterior mean time series in such a naive approach, we neglect the posterior uncertainty, which is a time-dependent function that contains a significant amount of information about the posterior estimate, something which is especially essential for CTNDSs. Besides, the posterior mean time series is simply a collection of the convex combinations of the pointwise maximum likelihood estimates and prior means from each time instant, which may not even be dynamically consistent with the underlying model. To resolve this issue, since the sampled trajectories from either (
3.1
) or (
3.7
) take into account the posterior uncertainty and temporal correlations, we may then assess the pathwise error through these sampled trajectories instead, which is more suitable for many circumstances, especially in understanding the dynamical behavior of the unobserved processes conditioned on the observations.

This subsection focuses on comparing the pathwise error in the posterior mean time series and that in the sampled trajectories. Towards this investigation, the standardized root-mean-square error (SRMSE) and the Pearson anomaly pattern correlation coefficient (Corr) are the two most widely used pathwise measurements in practice
[
45
,
173
,
174
,
175
,
176
,
177
]
. Let
\mathbf{y}(t)
denote the true
l
-dimensional time series of the unobservables and
\hat{\mathbf{y}}(t)
to represent a sampled trajectory of the unobservables, either from the filter-based forward- or smoother-based backward-conditional sampling formulas, (
3.1
) and (
3.7
), respectively. Furthermore, we let
\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}(t):=\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{\mathbf{y}}(t)\big|\mathcal{G}\right]
to denote the posterior mean time series from filtering or smoothing, depending on how we sampled
\hat{\mathbf{y}}(t)
(i.e.,
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}(t)}
or
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{F}_{T}^{\mathbf{x}}
, respectively), where the expectation is taken over all the ensembles (i.e., the samples denote the particles) at a fixed time instant. By Theorems
3.2
and
3.4
, these are indeed the filter and smoother posterior mean time series for
t\in[0,T]
, respectively. Moreover, let
\mathbf{c}(t)\in\mathbb{C}^{p}
be a stochastic process such that
\mathbf{c}(t)\in L^{1}([0,T];L^{2}\left(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P};\mathbb{C}^{p}\right))
, and define its discrete-time signal
\mathbf{c}_{\Delta t}:=\{\mathbf{c}^{j}_{\Delta t}=\mathbf{c}(t_{j})\}_{j=0,1,\ldots,J}
induced by the uniform time-discretization scheme outlined in the beginning of Section
2.5
. Then, we define the time average, the temporal covariance matrix, and standard deviation over
[0,T]
of the discretized process through the discrete measure defined by the partition as follows:

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{c}_{\Delta t}\rangle:=\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=0}^{J}\mathbf{c}(t_{j}),

(3.10)

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{c}_{\Delta t}):=\left(V_{jm}(\mathbf{c}_{\Delta t})\right)_{(J+1)\times(J+1)},\quad V_{jm}(\mathbf{c}_{\Delta t}):=(\mathbf{c}(t_{j})-\langle\mathbf{c}_{\Delta t}\rangle)^{\dagger}(\mathbf{c}(t_{m})-\langle\mathbf{c}_{\Delta t}\rangle)/J,

(3.11)

\displaystyle\mathrm{std}(\mathbf{c}_{\Delta t}):=\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{c}_{\Delta t}))^{1/2}.

(3.12)

Similarly, we can define analogous metrics for the continuous-time signal via integrals. Then, the SRMSE and the Corr between the true trajectory and the posterior mean time series are defined as (where we drop the subscript of
\Delta t
for notational simplicity):

\displaystyle\text{SRMSE}\big(\mathbf{y},\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\big):=\frac{1}{\mathrm{std}(\mathbf{y})}\sqrt{\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=0}^{J}\big\|\mathbf{y}^{j}-\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j}}\big\|_{2}^{2}},

(3.13)

\displaystyle\text{Corr}\big(\mathbf{y},\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\big):=\frac{\sum_{j=0}^{J}\big\langle\mathbf{y}^{j}-\langle\mathbf{y}\rangle,\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j}}-\langle\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\rangle\big\rangle_{2}}{\mathrm{std}(\mathbf{y})\mathrm{std}\big(\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\big)J},

(3.14)

where
\big\langle\boldsymbol{\cdot},\boldsymbol{\cdot}\big\rangle_{2}
denotes the usual Euclidean inner product over
\mathbb{C}^{l}
. These quantities depend on the equipartition of the time interval
[0,T]
and are sample-based (in time), thus contrasting the corresponding population quantities which use the true temporal ensemble average instead. Nevertheless, consistency, both in the weak and strong sense, can be achieved through standard asymptotic statistical analysis in the limit as
\Delta t\to 0^{+}
, assuming prior conditions hold. The same formulae apply for the SRMSE and Corr in the sampled trajectories by replacing
\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}
with
\hat{\mathbf{y}}
. Notably, uncertainty in the posterior estimates does not manifest in the pathwise measurements using the posterior mean time series but does when using sample trajectories.

The following theorem establishes a link between the SRMSE in the posterior mean time series and that in the sampled trajectory, whether from forward or backward sampling (
3.1
) and (
3.7
), with the latter accounting for both the average error and the uncertainty in the sampling process.

Theorem 3.6

(
Expected Sampling Squared Error
)
.

The expected squared error in
\hat{\mathbf{y}}
related to the truth
\mathbf{y}
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{SRMSE}(\mathbf{y},\hat{\mathbf{y}})^{2}\big|\mathcal{G}\right]&=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\mathrm{std}(\mathbf{y})^{2}J}\sum_{j=0}^{J}\big\|\mathbf{y}^{j}-\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j}\big\|_{2}^{2}\Big|\mathcal{G}\right]\\
&=\frac{1}{\mathrm{std}(\mathbf{y})^{2}}\Big(\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=0}^{J}\big\|\mathbf{y}^{j}-\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j}}\big\|_{2}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=0}^{J}\big\|\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j}}-\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j}\big\|_{2}^{2}\Big|\mathcal{G}\Big]\Big)\\
&=\mathrm{SRMSE}\big(\mathbf{y},\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\big)^{2}+\frac{\mathrm{std}\big(\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\big)^{2}}{\mathrm{std}(\mathbf{y})^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{SRMSE}\big(\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}},\hat{\mathbf{y}}\big)^{2}\big|\mathcal{G}\right].\end{split}

(3.15)

Proof of Theorem
3.6
.

(
3.15
) is easily obtained via simple algebra and by the linearity of the expectation, after using the polarization identity,

\big\|\mathbf{y}^{j}-\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j}\big\|_{2}^{2}=\big\|\mathbf{y}^{j}-\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}^{j}+\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}^{j}-\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j}\big\|_{2}^{2}=\big\|\mathbf{y}^{j}-\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}^{j}\big\|_{2}^{2}+\big\|\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}^{j}-\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j}\big\|_{2}^{2}+2\mathrm{Re}\big(\big\langle\mathbf{y}^{j}-\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}^{j},\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}^{j}-\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j}\big\rangle_{2}\big).

This result essentially relies on the fact that
\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j}|\mathcal{G}\right]=\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j}}
regardless of the sampling strategy, due to Theorems
3.2
and
3.4
and the uniqueness of solutions to the posterior state estimation and conditional sampling equations, i.e. for each
j=0,1,\ldots,J-1
we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j+1}|\mathcal{G}\right]=\begin{cases}\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j+1}\big|\mathcal{F}_{t_{j+1}}^{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{j}}\right]=\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{f}}}^{j+1}},&\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j}=\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{f}}}^{j},\ \mathcal{G}=\mathcal{F}_{t_{j+1}}^{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{j}}\\
\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j+1}|\mathcal{F}_{T}^{\mathbf{x}}\right]=\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}^{j+1}},&\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j}=\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}^{j},\ \mathcal{G}=\mathcal{F}_{T}^{\mathbf{x}},\end{cases}

with this equaling correspondingly either the filter or smoother posterior mean at
t_{j+1}
.
∎

This corresponds to the bias-variance decomposition
[
171
]
, where the first term in (
3.15
) represents the bias and the second term captures the variance. Notably, the bias component is precisely the square of the traditional SRMSE in the posterior mean time series (
3.13
). The variance term quantifies the additional uncertainties in the sampled trajectory, beyond the variability of the posterior mean time series, which arise from the corresponding posterior covariance tensor—whether from the filter or smoother—and are often overlooked in applications that consider only the posterior mean.

The next objective is to examine the difference in anomaly pattern correlation between the posterior mean time series and the sampled trajectories, as presented in the following theorem. Note that for this result we require that our scheme is accurate enough, in other words the time step is assumed to be small.

Theorem 3.7

(
Anomaly Pattern Correlation of Sampled Trajectories
)
.

Assuming
\Delta t
is sufficiently small, then the Corr coefficient between the sampled trajectory
\hat{\mathbf{y}}
and the truth
\mathbf{y}
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{Corr}(\mathbf{y},\hat{\mathbf{y}})&=\frac{\sum_{j=0}^{J}\big\langle\mathbf{y}^{j}-\langle\mathbf{y}\rangle,\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j}-\langle\hat{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\big\rangle_{2}}{\sqrt{\sum_{j=0}^{J}\left\|\mathbf{y}^{j}-\langle\mathbf{y}\rangle\right\|_{2}^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{j=0}^{J}\left\|\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j}-\langle\hat{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\right\|_{2}^{2}}}\\
&=\frac{\sum_{j=0}^{J}\Big(\left\langle\mathbf{y}^{j}-\langle\mathbf{y}\rangle,\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime,j}-\langle\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}\rangle\right\rangle_{2}+\big\langle\mathbf{y}^{j}-\langle\mathbf{y}\rangle,\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j}}-\langle\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\rangle\big\rangle_{2}\Big)}{\sqrt{\sum_{j=0}^{J}\big\|\mathbf{y}^{j}-\langle\mathbf{y}\rangle\big\|_{2}^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{j=0}^{J}\Big(\big\|\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j}}-\big\langle\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\big\rangle\big\|_{2}^{2}+\big\|\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime,j}-\langle\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}\rangle\big\|_{2}^{2}+2\mathrm{Re}\big(\big\langle\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j}}-\big\langle\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\big\rangle,\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime,j}-\langle\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}\rangle\big\rangle_{2}\big)\Big)}}\\
&=\eta\cdot\mathrm{Corr}\big(\mathbf{y},\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\big),\end{split}

(3.16)

where the constant
0\leq\eta\leq 1
is defined by

\eta:=\frac{\sqrt{\sum_{j=0}^{J}\big\|\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j}}-\langle\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\rangle\big\|_{2}^{2}}}{\sqrt{\sum_{j=0}^{J}\Big(\big\|\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j}}-\big\langle\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\big\rangle\big\|_{2}^{2}+\big\|\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime,j}-\langle\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}\rangle\big\|_{2}^{2}+2\mathrm{Re}\big(\big\langle\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j}}-\big\langle\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\big\rangle,\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime,j}-\langle\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}\rangle\big\rangle_{2}\big)\Big)}},

and
\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime,j}=\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j}-\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j}}
is the residual part of the sampled trajectory related to the posterior mean (where the overline denotes the conditional expectation with respect to the associated
\sigma
-algebra depending on the sampling strategy).

Proof of Theorem
3.7
.

Simple algebra and the bilinearity of the Euclidean inner product give the following identity:

\big\langle\mathbf{y}^{j}-\langle\mathbf{y}\rangle,\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j}-\langle\hat{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\big\rangle_{2}=\big\langle\mathbf{y}^{j}-\langle\mathbf{y}\rangle,\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime,j}-\langle\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}\rangle\big\rangle_{2}+\big\langle\mathbf{y}^{j}-\langle\mathbf{y}\rangle,\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{j}}-\langle\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\rangle\big\rangle_{2}.

Using an appropriate polarization identity, similar to that used in the proof of Theorem
3.6
, we recover the first equality. Next, observe that the time series of
\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}
, according to (
6.64
) for the forward sampler and (
6.67
) for the backward sampler, satisfies a conditionally linear Gaussian SDE, which is conditionally independent of the truth
\mathbf{y}
(due to the independence of the stochastic forcings, i.e., their respective stochastic forcings are mutually independent Wiener processes), that also satisfies a conditionally linear Gaussian SDE. Hence, by conditional independence in the temporal direction,
\mathrm{Corr}(\mathbf{y},\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})\approx 0
, granted
\Delta t
is sufficiently small (or
J
is large enough) such that the sample-based quantity converges to the population one under the law of large numbers, with the convergence rate being the parametric one of
O(\sqrt{J})
; this is because even though the population-counterpart of their correlation is zero, this does not translate to the sample-based metric as well, unless
\Delta t
is sufficiently small. This fact is used to derive the second equality of (
3.16
).
∎

Theorem
3.7
shows that the anomaly pattern correlation between the sampled trajectory and the truth,
\mathrm{Corr}(\mathbf{y},\hat{\mathbf{y}})
, equals that between the truth and the posterior mean time series,
\mathrm{Corr}\big(\mathbf{y},\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\big)
, up to a multiplicative factor
\eta
which depends on the filter or smoother sampling uncertainty
\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}
. As this uncertainty increases, the denominator in
\eta
also increases, making
\eta
smaller. Since
\eta\leq 1
, we have
|\mathrm{Corr}(\mathbf{y},\hat{\mathbf{y}})|\leq|\mathrm{Corr}\big(\mathbf{y},\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\big)|
, as expected. Only in the limiting case where the posterior uncertainty approaches zero does
\mathrm{Corr}(\mathbf{y},\hat{\mathbf{y}})
converge to
\mathrm{Corr}\big(\mathbf{y},\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\big)
.

3.5
The Temporal Autocorrelation Function

The temporal autocorrelation function (ACF) measures how a signal correlates with a delayed version of itself as a function of delay
[
163
]
. For a stochastic process
\mathbf{u}
over
[0,T]
, its scalar temporal ACF is defined as

\mathrm{ACF}_{\mathbf{u}}(s):=\lim_{T^{\prime}\to T}\frac{1}{T^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{Cov}(\mathbf{u}(t),\mathbf{u}(t+s))\right)}{\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{Var}(\mathbf{u}(t))\right)}\mathrm{d}t.

(3.17)

This scalar ACF focuses on ”diagonal” behavior, assuming negligible inter-coordinate correlations
[
178
]
. A similar ACF in both the perfect and approximate models implies similar dynamics, at least in second-order statistics, quantified via spectral representations
[
179
]
. For CTNDSs with extreme events, higher-order statistics are crucial, so the ACF serves as a rough measure of predictability.

To quantify system memory and dynamics recovery, we compare the ACFs of sampled trajectories to the truth. Assuming weak- or wide-sense stationarity in the CGNS dynamics (e.g., it is sufficient to assume that the CGNS of processes enjoys (geometric) ergodicity
[
180
,
181
,
182
]
), the ACFs for the truth and posterior mean time series, as
T\to+\infty
, are

\displaystyle\mathrm{ACF}_{\mathbf{y}}(s)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\big\langle\mathrm{tr}\big(\mathbb{E}\left[(\mathbf{y}(t)-\overline{\mathbf{y}}_{\infty})(\mathbf{y}(t+s)-\overline{\mathbf{y}}_{\infty})^{\dagger}\right]\big)\big\rangle}{\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{Var}(\mathbf{y})_{\infty}\right)},

(3.18)

\displaystyle\mathrm{ACF}_{\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}}(s)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\big\langle\mathrm{tr}\big(\mathbb{E}\big[\big(\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}(t)-\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\infty}\big)\big(\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}(t+s)-\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\infty}\big)^{\dagger}\big]\big)\big\rangle}{\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{Var}\big(\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\big)_{\infty}\right)},

(3.19)

where
\overline{\mathbf{y}}_{\infty}
and
\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\infty}
are the equilibrium means, and
\mathrm{Var}(\mathbf{y})_{\infty}
and
\mathrm{Var}\big(\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\big)_{\infty}
are the equilibrium covariances; we highlight that the expectations and variances taken in this regime, in (
3.18
) and (
3.19
), are the total ones since we consider the dynamics at the equilibrium. In practice, the sample covariance tensor is used to compute the ACF. These expressions, as well as (
3.20
) in Theorem
3.8
, can be derived using Euclidean norms, the interchangeability of the expectation and trace operators, and the cyclic property of the trace. The following theorem compares the ACF of the posterior mean time series with that of the sampled trajectory.

Theorem 3.8

(
Temporal ACF of Samples
)
.

The temporal ACF of the sampled trajectory
\hat{\mathbf{y}}
can be calculated as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{ACF}_{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}(s)&:=\frac{\big\langle\mathrm{tr}\big(\mathbb{E}\left[(\hat{\mathbf{y}}(t)-\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\infty})(\hat{\mathbf{y}}(t+s)-\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\infty})^{\dagger}\right]\big)\big\rangle}{\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{Var}\big(\hat{\mathbf{y}}\big)_{\infty}\right)}\\
&=\frac{\big\langle\mathrm{tr}\big(\mathbb{E}\left[(\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}(t)+\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}(t)-\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\infty})(\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}(t+s)+\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}(t+s)-\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\infty})^{\dagger}\right]\big)\big\rangle}{\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{Var}\big(\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}+\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\big)_{\infty}\right)}\\
&=\frac{\big\langle\mathrm{tr}\big(\mathbb{E}\big[\big(\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}(t)-\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\infty}\big)\big(\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}(t+s)-\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\infty}\big)^{\dagger}\big]\big)\big\rangle}{\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{Var}(\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})_{\infty}\right)+\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{Var}\big(\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\big)_{\infty}\right)}+\frac{\big\langle\mathrm{tr}\big(\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}(t)\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}(t+s)^{\dagger}\right]\big)\big\rangle}{\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{Var}(\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})_{\infty}\right)+\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{Var}\big(\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\big)_{\infty}\right)}\\
&=\beta_{1}\mathrm{ACF}_{\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}}(s)+\beta_{2}\mathrm{ACF}_{\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}}(s),\end{split}

(3.20)

where the constants
0\leq\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\leq 1
are given by

\displaystyle\beta_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{Var}\big(\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\big)_{\infty}\right)}{\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{Var}(\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})_{\infty}\right)+\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{Var}\big(\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\big)_{\infty}\right)},

\displaystyle\beta_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{Var}(\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})_{\infty}\right)}{\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{Var}(\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})_{\infty}\right)+\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{Var}\big(\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\big)_{\infty}\right)},

and
\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}=1
, i.e.,
\mathrm{ACF}_{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}(s)
is a convex linear combination of
\mathrm{ACF}_{\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}}(s)
and
\mathrm{ACF}_{\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}}(s)
.

Proof of Theorem
3.8
.

The definition of the temporal ACF for the sampled trajectories again follows by the consistency Theorems
3.2
and
3.4
. Simple algebra and using the interchangeability of the expectation and trace operators, along with the stability property of the conditional expectation, measurability of the posterior statistics, wide-sense stationarity of the CGNS, and the Fubini-Tonelli theorem, yields the second and third equality, since the term

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\big\langle\mathrm{tr}\big(\mathbb{E}\big[\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}(t)\big(\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}(t+s)-\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\infty}\big)^{\dagger}\big]\big)\big\rangle+\big\langle\mathrm{tr}\big(\mathbb{E}\big[\big(\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}(t)-\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\infty}\big)\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}(t+s)^{\dagger}\big]\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt=\mathbb{E}\big[\big\langle\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}(t+s)^{\dagger}\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}(t)+\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}(t+s)^{\dagger}\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}(t)\big\rangle\big]

vanishes under these conditions. Specifically, under the aforementioned results and conditions, we have

\mathbb{E}\big[\big\langle\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}(t+s)^{\dagger}\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}(t)\big\rangle\big]=\big\langle\mathbb{E}\big[\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}(t+s)^{\dagger}\mathbb{E}\big[\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}(t)\big|\mathcal{G}\big]\big]\big\rangle=0,

and likewise for
\mathbb{E}\big[\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}(t+s)^{\dagger}\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}(t)\big\rangle\big]
. Notice how in the third equality we also used the fact that

\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{Var}\big(\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}+\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\big)_{\infty}\right)=\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{Var}(\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})_{\infty}\right)+\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{Var}\big(\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\big)_{\infty}\right)+2\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{Cov}\big(\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime},\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\big)_{\infty}\right),

where
\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{Cov}\big(\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime},\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\big)_{\infty}\right)=0
, again by the stability property of the conditional expectation and measurability of the posterior mean (by following a similar procedure as we did with the expectation).
∎

It is important to note that the temporal ACF of
\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}
is not zero because
\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}
satisfies a dynamical equation with a finite damping rate (see (
6.64
) and (
6.67
)), for the forward and backward sampling algorithms, respectively. This is very different from the anomaly pattern correlation coefficient in (
3.16
), for which
\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}
has no contribution (apart from the multiplicative factor). This shows why using the ACF is preferred since it incorporates uncertainty in the residual, which does not happen in lower-order statistics like Corr. It is also worth noting that the integration of the ACF is likewise a crucial quantity and is named the decorrelation time
[
164
,
163
]
, which measures the memory of the system. The decorrelation time of the sampled trajectory can thus be smaller, equal, or larger than that of the posterior mean time series, leading to nontrivial results.

All these arguments apply regardless of which sampling strategy we use, forward or backward. But, the ACF of the backward sampled trajectories should intuitively almost perfectly reproduce the truth while that associated with the filter or smoother posterior mean time series may contain significant biases, as we investigate in the numerical case study in Section
4
. This also justifies the fact that the posterior mean time series is not dynamically consistent with the truth, while the sampled trajectories take into account the uncertainty and its temporal dependence, which is a more suitable way as a pathwise surrogate for the true signal.

4
Numerical Case Study: A Nonlinear Physics-Constrained Stochastic Reduced-Order Model

In this section, we showcase an application of the CGNS framework through its optimal state estimation and sampling procedures. The main goal of this case study is to demonstrate how the optimal nonlinear smoother state estimation, and associated optimal nonlinear backward samples, are potent in the recovery of the non-Gaussian intermittent features present in the unobservable components of a partially observed model that fits the conditional Gaussian structure, and how they contrast their filter-based counterparts in doing so. This is established through a reduced-order triad-interaction climate model with cubic nonlinearity in the observables, as well as multiplicative and cross-interacting noise. Such a complex model also showcases the disruption to the commonly assumed to be true hierarchy in the sample-to-sample fluctuation component of the latent uncertainty which was discussed in Section
3.3
.

4.1
The Model

The following nonlinear stochastic triad-system mimics structural features of low-frequency variability in general circulation models with non-Gaussian features
[
183
]
, where the observable variables comprise the low-frequency modes, while the hidden variables define the climate modes of high-frequency. This model involves extremely nontrivial interactions both in the deterministic as well as stochastic dynamics, with quadratic interactions between the state variables, a cubic nonlinearity in the observable, correlated additive and multiplicative (CAM) noise, and noise cross-interaction. Furthermore, the quadratic dyad-interaction nonlinear terms, which model the nonlinear advection, conserve the quadratic energy of the system, thus imposing physical constraints on the system, which also induce the intermittent instability onto
u_{1}
by functioning as antidamping terms. This model is presented in what follows and fits the CGNS setting outlined in (
2.3
)–(
2.4
) for
\mathbf{x}:=u_{1}
and
\mathbf{y}:=(u_{2},u_{3})^{\mathtt{T}}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}u_{1}&=\left(\gamma_{1}u_{1}+I_{12}u_{1}u_{2}+I_{13}u_{1}u_{3}-cu_{1}^{3}+L_{12}u_{2}+L_{13}u_{3}+F_{1}(t)\right)\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt+\sigma_{1}\mathrm{d}W_{u_{1}}+\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\gamma_{2}}(L_{12}-I_{12}u_{1})\mathrm{d}W_{u_{2}}+\frac{\sigma_{3}}{\gamma_{3}}(L_{13}-I_{13}u_{1})\mathrm{d}W_{u_{3}},\end{split}

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}u_{2}

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{\gamma_{2}}{\varepsilon}u_{2}-L_{12}u_{1}+L_{23}u_{3}-I_{12}u_{1}^{2}+F_{2}(t)\right)\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}W_{u_{2}},

(4.2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}u_{3}

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{\gamma_{3}}{\varepsilon}u_{3}-L_{13}u_{1}-L_{23}u_{2}-I_{13}u_{1}^{2}+F_{3}(t)\right)\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\sigma_{3}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}W_{u_{3}},

(4.3)

where
\displaystyle c=\sum_{p=2}^{3}\frac{I_{1p}^{2}}{\gamma_{p}}>0
,
\gamma_{1},L_{12},L_{13},L_{23},I_{12},I_{13}\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}>0
.
The parameter
\epsilon\in(0,1]
denotes the timescale separation between the potentially slow (
u_{1}
) and fast (
u_{2}
,
u_{3}
) variables. CAM noise arises from applying stochastic mode reduction to a more complex system with multiscale features
[
184
]
. The cubic nonlinearity with CAM has been applied to describe several climate phenomena
[
183
]
. The coupling between
u_{2}
and
u_{3}
is purely linear and represented by a skew-symmetric term
\pm L_{23}
, indicating an oscillatory structure. However, nonlinear feedback from
u_{1}
influences the governing equations for
u_{2}
and
u_{3}
, with
u_{2}
and
u_{3}
acting as stochastic damping for
u_{1}
. When
u_{2}
and
u_{3}
increase, this anti-damping triggers extreme events in
u_{1}
. In turn, strong signals in
u_{1}
reduce the amplitude of
u_{2}
and
u_{3}
via nonlinear feedback terms involving
u_{1}^{2}
, rapidly suppressing their signals. The quadratic nonlinear terms in the equations conserve energy, which acts as a physical constraint
[
13
]
. This mirrors many turbulent systems, where quadratic nonlinearities transfer energy between modes or scales, while damping and external forcing dissipate and generate energy. This coupled structure also appears in turbulent ocean flows, where
u_{1}
represents geostrophically balanced components, and
u_{2}
and
u_{3}
model inertio-gravity waves
[
185
]
. The large-scale forcing terms
F_{p}(t)
for
p=1,2,3
represent external inputs, such as seasonal effects, decadal oscillations, or rapid small-scale fluctuations
[
186
,
187
]
.

In the following, a time series of
u_{1}
is observed and the goal is to study the recovery of the states of
u_{2}
and
u_{3}
. The following simulation and model parameters are adopted in this numerical test:

\displaystyle T=60,\quad\Delta t=10^{-3},\quad\gamma_{1}=1,\quad I_{12}=0.5,\quad I_{13}=0.5,\quad L_{12}=0.5,\quad L_{13}=0.5,\quad F_{1}=3,\quad\sigma_{1}=0.5,

\displaystyle\epsilon=1,\quad\gamma_{2}=1.2,\quad L_{23}=2,\quad F_{2}=0,\quad\sigma_{2}=1.2,

\displaystyle\gamma_{3}=0.5,\quad F_{3}=0,\quad\sigma_{3}=0.8.

4.2
Comparison for Filtering, Smoothing, and Sampling Solutions

Figures
4.1
–
4.3
demonstrate the performance of filtering, smoothing, forward sampling, and backward sampling methods. Panel (a) of Figure
4.1
shows the observed time series of
u_{1}
, which exhibits intermittency and contains many extreme events. Correspondingly, its PDF in Panel (b) is highly skewed with a one-sided fat tail. In Panels (c) and (e), the smoother state estimates are more accurate than those of the filter in two ways. First, the smoother posterior mean is much closer to the true state. Notably, the filter posterior mean misses most peak events when
u_{2}
and
u_{3}
are positive, which correspond to extreme events in
u_{1}
. Since
u_{2}
and
u_{3}
act as stochastic damping for
u_{1}
, they trigger the extreme events in
u_{1}
. Thus, the occurrence of intermittent instability in
u_{1}
is delayed relative to its triggers,
u_{2}
and
u_{3}
. Recovering
u_{2}
and
u_{3}
in real time using only past information (as in filtering) is challenging, while future observations are crucial for accurately capturing these triggers. Second, the posterior variance, which is an indicator of the estimated uncertainty, is significantly lower for the smoother, as it incorporates more observational information. Note that, if the sampled trajectories from the forward and backward sampling methods are shown, they will exactly cover the uncertainty regions illustrated in the figure, per the results of Theorem
3.2
and
3.4
. Finally, consistent with the pathwise behavior in Panels (b) and (d), the PDF from the smoother mean is closer to the true PDF than that of the filter, as shown in Panels (c) and (e). However, the PDF from the smoother mean does not fully match the true PDF, as the smoother mean still underestimates the variability of the signal, which is captured in the posterior uncertainty. Only the trajectories from backward sampling can generate PDFs that align with the true distribution, assuming they are sufficiently long.

Figure 4.1:
Performance of filtering, smoothing, forward sampling, and backward sampling methods using the reduced-order climate model in (
4.1
)–(
4.3
). Panels (a)–(b): Observed time series of
u_{1}
and its corresponding PDF. Panels (c)–(d): State estimation of
u_{2}
with filtering and smoothing, along with the corresponding PDFs. Panels (e)–(f): State estimation of
u_{3}
with filtering and smoothing, and the corresponding PDFs. The PDFs correspond to the full-time-length signal, i.e., for
t\in[0,60]
.

Figure
4.2
presents a statistical comparison of state estimation using different approaches. Consistent with the findings on PDF recovery, the smoother mean captures the autocorrelation function (ACF; Panels (a)–(d)) and power spectral density (PSD; Panels (e)–(h)) more effectively than the filtering mean. The filtering mean misses many peak events, leading to noticeable errors in both the decay rate during the initial period of the ACF and the low-frequency PSD. Similarly, trajectories from forward sampling, based on the filter estimate, exhibit comparable issues in their ACF and PSD (not shown here). In contrast, the smoother mean accurately captures the statistics with much smaller errors. The trajectories from backward sampling further account for the variability provided by the smoother variance. They are dynamically consistent with the true values and can yield unbiased statistics, provided they are sufficiently long. The numerical evidence presented here verifies the analysis in Section
3.5
. Panels (i)–(j) display the correlation coefficients between the true time series and the estimates from different methods. The smoother mean time series shows a higher correlation with the true series than the filter mean, for both regular and extreme events. Although the sampled trajectories demonstrate improved statistical capture, their correlations with the truth are weaker. This is not surprising, as the enhancement in statistical accuracy arises from incorporating additional random variability from the posterior uncertainty, which naturally decreases the pathwise similarity to the true series. The conclusion is also consistent with the analysis in Section
3.4
(specifically the observation that
|\mathrm{Corr}(\mathbf{y},\hat{\mathbf{y}})|\leq|\mathrm{Corr}\big(\mathbf{y},\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}\big)|
, since
\eta\leq 1
).

Figure 4.2:
Statistical comparison between the state estimation using different approaches. Panels (a)–(d): Comparison of the autocorrelation function (ACF). Panels (e)–(h): Comparison of the power spectral density (PSD). Panels (i)–(j): Correlation coefficient between the true time series and the recovered ones from different methods. All comparisons are based on a time series of 60 time units. The SRMSE leads to a similar conclusion as the Corr and is therefore omitted here.

4.3
Analysis of the Uncertainty Levels in Different State Estimation Methods

Finally, in Figure
4.3
, we show the time evolution of the expected maximal eigenvalues for the damping (Panels (a)–(b)) and fluctuation (Panels (c)–(d)) components of the sampling uncertainty emerging in the unobservable variables. This figure should be understood based on the discussions following Theorems
3.1
and
3.3
, and by extending the exposition provided in Section
3.3
. For reference, the unconditional damping and noise feedbacks are extracted from the time evolution equation for the latent dynamics of the CGNS, (
2.4
), while the damping and fluctuation components for the forward filter-based and backward smoother-based sampling procedures can be retrieved from their corresponding residual equations, (
6.64
) and (
6.67
), by looking at their drift and diffusion coefficients, respectively. Note that the x-axis (time axis) is plotted in the logarithm scale. The solution will arrive near the equilibrium within a short period, regardless of the initial value. All subsequent discussions are based on the equilibrium state.

We start with Panels (c)–(d). Since the backward-sampling noise feedback is always no larger than the unconditional and filter-based ones (in the Loewner partial ordering sense), the maximal eigenvalues for
\mathbf{B}
will at all times be less than the respective ones for
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}
and
\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}
, with all being nonnegative due to the nonnegative-definiteness of the noise amplitudes. Yet, as already alluded in Sections
3.1
and
3.3
, it is not always that this hierarchy holds as well between these components for the filter-based sampling and unconditional forward run of the system, as is indicated in Panel (d). Since the black curve, corresponding to the expected minimum eigenvalue of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}-\mathbf{B}-\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}
, goes below zero, this explicitly shows that the difference between these matrices is an indefinite matrix after the fast and exponentially-reached relaxation time. However, it is known that the overall uncertainty in the optimal filter estimated state (e.g., the posterior variance) is all times smaller than the unconditional or marginal uncertainty of
\mathbf{y}=(u_{2},u_{3})^{\mathtt{T}}
. So while the results shown in Panel (d) might at first seem counterintuitive, it is essential to observe that the correction term in (
2.13
), i.e., the terms other than
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}
, which accounts for the observance of data stemming from
\mathbf{x}
, does not just depend on the optimal Kalman gain interacting with the uncertainties in the innovation, but is also influenced by the damping components which help, under the Bayesian framework, to mitigate the uncertainty in the latent state variable, regardless of the quality of the current measurement. These corrective terms appear in the time evolution of the posterior covariance matrices and help reduce the overall uncertainty. They correspond to nontrivial damping feedbacks, which we showcase in Panels (a)–(b) for both the forward and backward sampling and the unconditional dynamics. Note that the damping feedback in the forward sampler is exactly the damping that helps stabilize the filter mean per (
3.4
), while the smoother-based one is the damping which controls the smoother covariance per (
2.16
), instead. As such, the overall uncertainty, while consistently decreasing with increasing incorporation of information, its constituents might have nontrivial temporal profiles and seemingly showcase counterintuitive results if only a marginal component of the uncertainty is taken into consideration. This is precisely illustrated by the fact that now the filter-based damping is always stronger compared to the unconditional one, which under a bias-variance trade-off-type argument reveals why the uncertainty indeed decreases by forward sampling the unobservable variables. Specifically, note how when the filter-based noise feedback’s maximal eigenvalues go above the unconditional ones, the respective damping component of the uncertainty increases to counteract this. As such, while the observation incorporation might increase the sample-to-sample fluctuations, the overall unpredictability in the estimated state will nonetheless diminish due to the strengthened damping.

Figure 4.3:
Time evolution of the spectrum for the components of the sample-to-sample uncertainty with respect to the different procedures of simulating the unobservable variables. Panels (a)–(b): Comparison with regards to the expected real part of the maximal and minimal eigenvalues of the damping feedback in the unconditional forward run of (
4.2
)–(
4.3
) and the conditional forward and backward optimal nonlinear sampling of
(u_{2},u_{3})
. The x-axis is logarithmically scaled. Panels (c)–(d): Same as (a)–(b), but instead concerning the fluctuation part of the uncertainty, i.e., the noise feedback matrices. The time evolution of the expected maximal and minimal eigenvalues is also shown for the matrix difference between the unconditional and filter-based sampling noise coefficients.

5
Conclusion

This paper develops a martingale-free approach that facilitates the understanding of CGNS. The martingale-free method provides a tractable approach to proving the time evolution of conditional statistics by deriving results through time discretization schemes, with the continuous-time regime obtained via a formal limiting process as the discretization time step approaches zero. This has the consequence of a natural unification between the two settings. Furthermore, this discretized approach further allows for the development of analytic formulae for optimal posterior sampling algorithms of the unobserved state variables with correlated noise, which is crucial in settings where uncertainty from one subset of the phase space feeds into the other. These analytic tools are particularly valuable for studying extreme events and intermittency and apply to high-dimensional systems. Moreover, the approach improves the understanding of different state estimation and sampling methods in characterizing the uncertainty. The effectiveness of the methods is demonstrated through a physics-constrained, triad-interaction reduced-order climate model with cubic nonlinearity and state-dependent cross-interacting noise. The model simulation helps understand the skill of state estimation, especially for recovering the highly non-Gaussian features of intermittency and extreme events.

6
Appendix

6.1
Preliminary Definitions and Lemmas

To prove most of the results in this work, we first need to define the so-called Reynolds decomposition of a stochastic quantity.

Definition 6.1 (Mean-Fluctuation Decomposition).

The mean-fluctuation decomposition of a complex random variable
\mathbf{z}
at a fixed time instant, also known as the Reynolds decomposition, is given by
[
186
,
188
]
:

\mathbf{z}=\overline{\mathbf{z}}+\mathbf{z}^{\prime},

(6.1)

where
\overline{\mathbf{z}}=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{z}\right]
is the ensemble average or mean of
\mathbf{z}
and
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}
, with
\overline{\mathbf{z}^{\prime}}=\mathbf{0}
, is the residual or the deviations around the expectation value (or fluctuations). The covariance tensor of
\mathbf{z}
is given compactly by
\overline{\mathbf{z}^{\prime}(\mathbf{z}^{\prime})^{\dagger}}
.

Furthermore, we state the following three necessary lemmas that are extensively used to establish the assertions stated in this article. The first one concerns itself with how affine transformations affect Gaussian distributions.

Lemma 6.2 (Affine Transformation of Normal Variable).

The distribution of a multivariate Gaussian random variable is invariant under affine transformations. In other words, for any constant vector
\mathbf{c}
and constant nonzero matrix
\mathbf{G}
, if
\mathbf{z}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{z})}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{}},\mathbf{R}_{\text{}})
, then
\mathbf{G}\mathbf{z}+\mathbf{c}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{G}\mathbf{z})}(\mathbf{G}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{}}+\mathbf{c},\mathbf{G}\mathbf{R}_{\text{}}\mathbf{G}^{\dagger})
.

Proof of Lemma
6.2
.

This result is proved in Proposition 3.1 of Eaton
[
189
]
.
∎

The second one explicitly showcases how the Bayesian update of a conditional and marginal normal behaves. In this regard, and for the remainder of this work, we denote the PDF of a multivariate
\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{u})
-dimensional Gaussian distribution with mean
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{}}
and covariance matrix
\mathbf{R}_{\text{}}
by
\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{u})}\left(\mathbf{u};\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{}},\mathbf{R}_{\text{}}\right)
.

Lemma 6.3 (Bayesian Update of a Gaussian).

For two Gaussian distributions, with
k=\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{u}_{1})
and
l=\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{u}_{2})
, we have

\int_{\mathbb{C}^{k}}\mathcal{N}_{l}\left(\mathbf{u}_{2};\mathbf{F}\mathbf{u}_{1}+\mathbf{b},\mathbf{R}_{\text{$2$}}\right)\mathcal{N}_{k}\left(\mathbf{u}_{1};\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{$1$}},\mathbf{R}_{\text{$1$}}\right)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}_{1}=\mathcal{N}_{l}\big(\mathbf{u}_{2};\mathbf{F}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{$1$}}+\mathbf{b},\mathbf{F}\mathbf{R}_{\text{$1$}}\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{$2$}}\big),

for appropriately sized vectors and matrices, i.e.,
\mathbf{b}
and
\mathbf{F}
are assumed to be conformable.

Proof of Lemma
6.3
.

This result is proved in Section 2.3.3 of Bishop
[
190
]
(formulated through precision matrices, with the precision matrix defined as the inverse of the covariance matrix).
∎

Finally, the third lemma, also known as the theorem on normal correlations, is a fundamental result in conditional autoregressive modeling. Here we use the notation
p(\boldsymbol{\cdot})
to denote the law or density (PDF), conditional or not, of the probability distribution
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\cdot})
corresponding to the random variable or vector of interest.

Lemma 6.4 (Conditional Distribution of Joint Normal or Theorem on Normal Correlation).

Let the following random vector be Gaussian:

\mathbf{z}=(\mathbf{z}_{1}^{\mathtt{T}},\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\mathtt{T}})^{\mathtt{T}},

with mean
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{}}
and covariance
\mathbf{R}_{\text{}}
, where

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{}}=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{$1$}}\\
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{$2$}}\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{R}_{\text{}}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{R}_{\text{$11$}}&\mathbf{R}_{\text{$12$}}\\
\mathbf{R}_{\text{$21$}}&\mathbf{R}_{\text{$22$}}\end{pmatrix}.

Then the following conditional distribution is Gaussian:

\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{z}_{1}\big|\mathbf{z}_{2}=\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{2}\big)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{z}_{1})}\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{}}},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}_{\text{}}}\right),

with a mean dependent on the mean and state of the conditioner, while the covariance matrix is independent of it, or,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{}}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{$1$}}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{$12$}}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{22}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{$2$}}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{R}_{\text{}}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{\text{$11$}}-\mathbf{R}_{\text{$12$}}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{22}\mathbf{R}_{\text{$21$}}.

Proof of Lemma
6.4
.

This is proved in Section 4.1 of Eaton
[
189
]
and Sections 2.3.1–2.3.2 of Bishop
[
190
]
. It also corresponds to Theorems 13.1 and 13.2 in Liptser & Shiryaev
[
69
]
.
∎

6.2
Proof of Theorem
2.2

Proof of Theorem
2.2
.

This is Theorem 12.7 in Liptser & Shiryaev
[
69
]
, which is the multi-dimensional analog of Theorems 12.1 and 12.3. For the analogous result in the case of discrete time, see Theorem 13.4, which outlines the corresponding optimal recursive nonlinear filter difference equations, with the respective sufficient assumptions given in Subchapter 13.2.1. Thorough details are also provided in
[
126
]
for the continuous-time case. Here, we instead provide a proof under our unified discrete- and continuous-time framework without the use of martingales.

We consider the discretized equations (
2.10
)–(
2.11
). In a similar manner, we then provide the recurrence relations that the fluctuation terms satisfy when the mean-fluctuation decomposition (
6.1
) is employed in (
2.3
)–(
2.4
) (with the expectation being the conditional one under the observation of the discrete data
\mathbf{x}^{0},\ldots,\mathbf{x}^{j}
for
j\in\{0,\ldots,J\}
):

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{\prime,j+1}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{x}^{j+1}-\overline{\mathbf{x}^{j+1}}=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{y}^{\prime,j}\Delta t+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{x},j}\sqrt{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{1}^{j}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{x},j}\sqrt{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{2}^{j},

(6.2)

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}^{\prime,j+1}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{y}^{j+1}-\overline{\mathbf{y}^{j+1}}=\mathbf{y}^{\prime,j}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{y}^{\prime,j}\Delta t+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{y},j}\sqrt{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{1}^{j}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{y},j}\sqrt{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{2}^{j},

(6.3)

where since during averaging we conditioning on
\mathbf{x}^{0},\ldots,\mathbf{x}^{j}
, terms involving
\mathbf{x}^{j}
should not appear in the equations for the fluctuations due to the form of (
2.10
)–(
2.11
). We now turn our attention to the joint distribution of
(\mathbf{x}^{j+1},\mathbf{y}^{j+1})
, when conditioning on
\mathbf{x}^{s},\ s\leq j
. During the proof of Theorem 13.6 of Liptser & Shiryaev
[
69
]
it is proved via induction and the uniqueness of conditional characteristic functions
[
137
]
that the conditional joint distribution

\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j+1}\leq\boldsymbol{\alpha},\mathbf{x}^{j+1}\leq\boldsymbol{\beta}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},\ s\leq j\big),

(6.4)

for sequences
\{(\mathbf{x}^{j},\mathbf{y}^{j})\}_{j=0,1,\ldots,J}
governed by discrete-time systems in the form of (
2.10
)–(
2.11
), is
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. Gaussian,
\forall j=0,\ldots,J-1
(after using the exchangeability (in law) property of jointly distributed Gaussian random vectors by virtue of Lemma
6.2
and permutation matrices). We then aim to describe the mean and covariance matrix of this distribution. First, in light of (
2.10
)–(
2.11
), (
6.4
), and Lemma
6.2
(as to isolate the marginals), a bit of linear algebra provides the following marginalized conditionally Gaussian distributions:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{x}^{j+1}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}_{k}\big(\mathbf{x}^{j}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x},j})\Delta t,(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\Delta t\big),

(6.5)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j+1}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}_{l}\big(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y},j})\Delta t,\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j})\Delta t\big),

(6.6)

where
\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}_{l}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j},\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j})
by Theorem
2.1
. Note again how due to the nature of the EM discretization scheme being used, terms of order higher than
O(\Delta t)
have been dropped in the Gaussian statistics appearing in (
6.5
)–(
6.6
). As for the cross-covariance tensor,
\mathrm{Cov}\left(\mathbf{x}^{j+1},\mathbf{y}^{j+1}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\right):=\overline{\mathbf{x}^{\prime,j+1}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime,j+1})^{\dagger}}
, where the overline denotes taking the expectation when conditioned on the observations
\mathbf{x}^{0},\ldots,\mathbf{x}^{j}
, we multiply (
6.2
) by the Hermitian transpose of (
6.3
) from the right, which gives

\mathrm{Cov}\left(\mathbf{x}^{j+1},\mathbf{y}^{j+1}|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\right):=\overline{\mathbf{x}^{\prime,j+1}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime,j+1})^{\dagger}}=(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j})\Delta t,

(6.7)

where again terms of order higher than
O(\Delta t)
have been dropped, while the terms of order
O(\sqrt{\Delta t})
disappear since by the law of total expectation and properties of the Gaussian random noises we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}^{j}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime,j+1})^{\dagger}\big|\mathcal{F}_{t_{j}}^{\mathbf{x}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}^{j}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime,j+1})^{\dagger}\big|\mathcal{F}_{t_{j}}^{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{j+1}}\right]\Big|\mathcal{F}_{t_{j}}^{\mathbf{x}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}^{j}\big|\mathcal{F}_{t_{j}}^{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{j+1}}\right](\mathbf{y}^{\prime,j+1})^{\dagger}\Big|\mathcal{F}_{t_{j}}^{\mathbf{x}}\right]=0,\ \forall m=1,2,

where
\mathcal{F}_{t_{j}}^{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{j+1}}
denotes the join
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{t_{j}}^{\mathbf{x}}\vee\mathcal{F}^{\mathbf{y}^{j+1}}:=\sigma\Big(\mathcal{F}_{t_{j}}^{\mathbf{x}}\cup\sigma(\mathbf{y}^{j+1})\Big)\supseteq\mathcal{F}_{t_{j}}^{\mathbf{x}}
. As such, by (
6.4
) and (
6.5
)–(
6.7
), the following joint distribution can be formed

\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{j+1}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}_{l+k}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{j+1}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\right],\mathrm{Var}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{j+1}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)\right),

(6.8)

where

\begin{gathered}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{j+1}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\right]=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y},j})\Delta t\\
\mathbf{x}^{j}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x},j})\Delta t\end{pmatrix},\\
\mathrm{Var}\left(\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{j+1}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\right)=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\mathbf{H}^{j}\Delta t)&\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\Delta t\\
\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\Delta t&(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\Delta t\end{pmatrix},\end{gathered}

(6.9)

for

\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}:=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j}\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1},\quad\mathbf{H}^{j}:=\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j}),

with
\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}
denoting the identity matrix of size
l\times l
. As such, in light of Lemma
6.4
and (
6.8
)–(
6.9
), by further conditioning on
\mathbf{x}^{j+1}
(meaning we have additionally observed
\mathbf{x}^{j+1}
), then we have that,

\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j+1}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j+1\big)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}_{l}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j+1},\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j+1}),

where we have the following recursive relations for the conditional filter mean and covariance, which essentially are the discrete-time optimal nonlinear filter state estimation equations:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j+1}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y},j})\Delta t+\mathbf{K}^{j}(\mathbf{x}^{j+1}-\mathbf{x}^{j}-(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x},j})\Delta t),

(6.10)

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j+1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\big(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j}-\mathbf{K}^{j}((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j})\big)\Delta t,

(6.11)

where

\mathbf{K}^{j}:=((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger})\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1},

(6.12)

which for the remainder of this work is called the Kalman gain matrix (see subsequent discussion), evaluated at
t_{j}
. Taking then the limit as
\Delta t\to 0^{+}
, under the EM convergence framework we have formalized, results in the recovery of the continuous-time filter differential equations for the Gaussian statistics of the posterior distribution given in (
2.12
)–(
2.13
), when
t\in[0,T]
.

As for the uniqueness and continuity of solutions to (
2.12
)–(
2.13
) (as well as their continuous dependence on the initial conditions and additional model parameters), the details can be found in Kolodziej
[
126
]
, but essentially these follow by the fact that
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}(t)
satisfies a random ordinary differential equation linear in
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}(t)

[
148
,
149
,
150
,
151
]
, while
\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(t)
satisfies a random Riccati equation
[
152
,
153
,
154
]
, with matrix-valued coefficients and forcings that satisfy the conditions of the random variant of the Picard-Lindelöf theorem (under our assumed regularity conditions)
[
148
,
151
,
149
]
. An alternative approach to the existence, uniqueness, and continuity of the filter Gaussian statistics is provided in the corresponding theorems from Liptser & Shiryayev
[
68
,
69
