]
.

For the final statement of the theorem, having now transitioned to the limit in continuous-time, the result can be established via elementary results from the theory of differential equations. We first define the random time

\tau:={\inf}\{t\in[0,T]:\mathrm{det}(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(t))=0\},

with the convention that
\tau=T
if
\underset{t\in[0,T]}{\inf}\{\mathrm{det}(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(t))\}>0
. This is the smallest random time on which the filter covariance matrix becomes singular, and by the virtue of the filter statistics being adapted to the filtration
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\mathbf{x}}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
, continuous in time, and by continuity of the determinant over
\mathbb{C}^{l\times l}
, then
\tau
is a predictable (previsible) stopping time
[
191
]
. As a result, for any
t<\min\{\tau,T\}
, the matrices
\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t)
are well-defined, and as such over this period we have (
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.)

\mathbf{0}_{l\times l}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l})=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(t)\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t))=\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}}{\mathrm{d}t}(t)\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t)+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(t)\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}}{\mathrm{d}t}(t),

or,

\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}}{\mathrm{d}t}(t)=-\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t)\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}}{\mathrm{d}t}(t)\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t).

Using then (
2.13
), we have that for
t<\min\{\tau,T\}
, the inverse of the filter covariance matrix satisfies the following random Riccati differential equation:

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t)=-\big(\mathbf{A}^{\dagger}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}\mathbf{A}-(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}})^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}\big)\mathrm{d}t,

(6.13)

where the auxiliary matrices in (
6.13
) are defined as:

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}(t,\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle:=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y}}(t,\mathbf{x})-(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})(t,\mathbf{x})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}(t,\mathbf{x})\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{x})\in\mathbb{C}^{l\times l},

(6.14)

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}(t,\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle:=(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})(t,\mathbf{x})-(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})(t,\mathbf{x})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}(t,\mathbf{x})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})(t,\mathbf{x})\in\mathbb{C}^{l\times l}.

(6.15)

Now, by
\mathrm{det}(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t))=\mathrm{det}(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(t))^{-1}
, continuity of the determinant, and
\tau
being a predictable stopping time, it is necessary that the elements of the matrix
\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t)
must increase as
t\uparrow\tau
. Nonetheless, it is actually possible to show that they are
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. bounded.

We first prove the auxiliary result of,

\mathbf{B}\succeq\mathbf{0}_{l\times l}\quad(\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.}),

(6.16)

i.e.,
\mathbf{B}
is nonnegative-definite. Let
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{1}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}_{d}(\mathbf{0}_{d\times 1},\mathbf{I}_{d})
and
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{2}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}_{r}(\mathbf{0}_{r\times 1},\mathbf{I}_{r})
, with
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{1}\perp\!\!\!\!\perp\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{2}
, and define
\boldsymbol{\theta}:=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}_{1}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{2}
and
\boldsymbol{\xi}:=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}_{1}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{2}
. Then, by Theorem
2.1
, we have

\mathrm{Cov}\big(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\boldsymbol{\xi}\big):=\mathbf{B}=(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})(t,\mathbf{x})-(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})(t,\mathbf{x})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}(t,\mathbf{x})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})(t,\mathbf{x}),

and as a covariance matrix it is necessarily nonnegative-definite.

By taking now the trace of (
6.13
) and using its continuity and linearity to yield
\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t))=\mathrm{d}(\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t)))
, it follows then by the invariance of the trace under cyclic permutations of products that

\mathrm{d}(\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t)))=-2\mathrm{tr}\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t)\mathbf{A}\big)+\mathrm{tr}\big((\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}})^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}\big)-\mathrm{tr}\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}\big),

and since
\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}\succeq\mathbf{0}_{l\times l}
by (
6.16
), for
t<\min\{\tau,T\}
, we end up with

\mathrm{d}(\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t)))\leq-2\mathrm{tr}\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t)\mathbf{A}\big)+M\left\|(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}\right\|_{2}^{2},

since by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality on the Frobenius inner product and the equivalence of norms in finite-dimensional spaces we have that
\exists M>0
such that

\mathrm{tr}\big((\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}})^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}\big)=\mathrm{tr}\big(\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}\big)^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}\big)\leq M\left\|(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

After integration over
[0,t]
for
t<\min\{\tau,T\}
we then have

\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t))\leq\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(0))+M\int_{0}^{t}\left\|(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1/2}(s,\mathbf{x})\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}(s,\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}^{2}\mathrm{d}s-2\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{tr}\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(s)\mathbf{A}(s,\mathbf{x})\big)\mathrm{d}s.

(6.17)

Now, since over this interval we have
\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t)\succ\mathbf{0}_{l\times l}
, then

\left\|\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t)\right\|^{2}_{2}\leq\rho(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t))^{2}\leq\sum_{\mathclap{\mu(t)\in\lambda(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t))}}\mu(t)^{2}=\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t))^{2},

(6.18)

where
\rho(\boldsymbol{\cdot})
denotes the spectral radius and
\lambda(\boldsymbol{\cdot})
denotes the spectrum of a linear operator. Furthermore, by the sub-multiplicativity of the induced Euclidean norm, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(0))+M\int_{0}^{t}\left\|(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1/2}(s,\mathbf{x})\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}(s,\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}^{2}\mathrm{d}s&\\
&\hskip-113.81102pt\leq\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(0))+M\int_{0}^{t}\left\|(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1/2}(s,\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}(s,\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}^{2}\mathrm{d}s:=C_{1},\end{split}

(6.19)

where
C_{1}<+\infty
(
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.), by assumptions
(2)
,
(5)
, and
\mathbb{P}\left(\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(0))<+\infty\right)=1
. Now, again by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality on the Frobenius inner product and the equivalence of norms in finite-dimensional spaces we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}-\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{tr}\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(s)\mathbf{A}(s,\mathbf{x})\big)\mathrm{d}s\leq\int_{0}^{t}|\mathrm{tr}\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(s)\mathbf{A}(s,\mathbf{x})\big)|\mathrm{d}s\leq M\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(s)\right\|_{2}\left\|\mathbf{A}(s,\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}\mathrm{d}s,\end{split}

(6.20)

and so by combining (
6.18
)–(
6.20
) with (
6.17
), and applying Young’s inequality we get

\left\|\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t)\right\|^{2}_{2}\leq 2C^{2}_{1}+8M^{2}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(s)\right\|_{2}\left\|\mathbf{A}(s,\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{2}.

Now, as a positive function,
\displaystyle\exists\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(s)\right\|^{2}_{2}\mathrm{d}s\in(0,+\infty]
, and as such, in the generalized sense, Cauchy-Schwarz yields

\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(s)\right\|_{2}\left\|\mathbf{A}(s,\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{2}\leq\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(s)\right\|^{2}_{2}\mathrm{d}s\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\mathbf{A}(s,\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}^{2}\mathrm{d}s,

where
\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\mathbf{A}(s,\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\leq\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\mathbf{A}(t,\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}^{2}\mathrm{d}t<+\infty
since by Young’s inequality and the sub-multiplicativity of the induced Euclidean norm, we have

\displaystyle C_{2}:=\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\mathbf{A}(t,\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\mathrm{d}t+2\int_{0}^{T}\left\|(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}(\left\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\right\|_{2}^{4}+\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y}}\right\|_{2}^{4})\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\big(\left\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{y}}\right\|_{2}^{4}+\left\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{y}}\right\|_{2}^{4}+\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y}}\right\|_{2}^{4}\big)\mathrm{d}t<+\infty,

as a result of assumptions
(2)
,
(5)
, and
(11)
. Putting all these results together, we retrieve

\left\|\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t)\right\|^{2}_{2}\leq 2C^{2}_{1}+8M^{2}C_{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(s)\right\|^{2}_{2}\mathrm{d}s,

where by Grönwall’s inequality we have

\left\|\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t)\right\|^{2}_{2}\leq 2C_{1}^{2}+16TC_{1}^{2}M^{2}C_{2}e^{8TM^{2}C_{2}}<+\infty,\ \forall t<\min\{\tau,T\}.

Using once more the equivalence of norms, in this case for
\left\|\boldsymbol{\cdot}\right\|_{2}
and the
L^{\infty,\infty}
element-wise norm,
\left\|\mathbf{V}\right\|_{\infty,\infty}:=\underset{i,j}{\max}\{|V_{ij}|\}
, then

\left\|\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t)\right\|^{2}_{\infty,\infty}\lesssim\left\|\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t)\right\|^{2}_{2}\lesssim 1+e^{8TM^{2}C_{2}}<+\infty,

which proves that the elements of
\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t)
remain
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. bounded as
t\uparrow\tau
. But, because of this boundedness of elements, as to not contradict the definition of
\tau
(the elements of the matrix
\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t)
must increase as
t\uparrow\tau
), it is then necessary that
\tau=T
, and as such
\mathbb{P}(\tau<T)=0
. We note here that we have essentially proved the boundedness of the elements and spectral or Euclidean norm of
\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t)
, which we can also show for the filter covariance matrix itself,
\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(t)
, with a similar procedure. Similarly, we can also show that
\mathbb{P}\big(\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}^{2}\mathrm{d}t<+\infty\big)
; details are included in the work of Kolodziej
[
126
]
.
∎

6.3
Proof of Theorem
2.3

The following lemmas are needed to derive the optimal nonlinear smoother state estimation backward equations. The first lemma provides some concentration inequalities, specifically tail bounds on the spectrum norm, and by extension the spectral radius, of some auxiliary matrices of interest. These are important for the expansion up to leading-order terms of expressions of the form
(\mathbf{I}_{p}\pm\mathbf{V}\Delta t)^{-1}
for
\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{C}^{p\times p}
through the Neumann series associated with this inverse.

Lemma 6.5 (Tail Bounds for some Spectral Norms).

Let the regularity conditions
(1)
–
(13)
to hold, and adopt the temporal discretization scheme that was formulated at the beginning of Section
2.5
. We further define the following auxiliary matrices:

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}(t,\mathbf{x}):=\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t)(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y}}(t,\mathbf{x})\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(t)+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(t)(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y}}(t,\mathbf{x}))^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})(t,\mathbf{x})),

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}(t,\mathbf{x}):=(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}(t,\mathbf{x})\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{x})\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(t)(\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,\mathbf{x}))^{\dagger},

where
\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x}}:=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j}(t,\mathbf{x})+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})(t,\mathbf{x})\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}(t)
. Then the following are true:

\displaystyle\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\left(\underset{0\leq j\leq J}{\max}\left\{\left\|\mathbf{H}^{j}\Delta t\right\|_{2}\right\}\geq 1\right)=0\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\left(\underset{0\leq j\leq J}{\max}\left\{\left\|\mathbf{L}^{j}\Delta t\right\|_{2}\right\}\geq 1\right)=0.

(6.21)

Proof of Lemma
6.5
.

We start with the tail result concerning
\mathbf{H}
in (
6.21
). Define

B_{j}(\Delta t):=\left\{\omega\in\Omega:\left\|\mathbf{H}^{j}(\omega)\Delta t\right\|_{2}\geq 1\right\}=\left\{\omega\in\Omega:\left\|\mathbf{H}(t_{j},\mathbf{x}(t_{j},\omega))\Delta t\right\|_{2}\geq 1\right\},

for each
j=0,1,\ldots,J
. Then, for
A(\Delta t):=\left\{\omega\in\Omega:\underset{0\leq j\leq J}{\max}\left\{\left\|\mathbf{H}^{j}(\omega)\Delta t\right\|_{2}\right\}\geq 1\right\}
, it immediately follows that

A(\Delta t)=\bigcup_{j=0}^{J}B_{j}(\Delta t),\ \forall J\in\mathbb{N}.

By Boole’s inequality, we then have
\mathbb{P}(A(\Delta t))\leq\sum_{j=0}^{J}\mathbb{P}(B_{j}(\Delta t))
. While this upper bound might seem rather crude, intuitively, for smaller values of
\Delta t
, this domination gets tighter. We now look at a specific
\mathbb{P}(B_{j}(\Delta t))
. We first define the so called self-adjoint dilation matrix from operation theory
[
192
]
, also known as the Jordan–Wielandt matrix in perturbation theory and other areas
[
193
,
161
]
. This can be defined as an operator from
\mathbb{C}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}
to
\mathbb{C}^{(d_{1}+d_{2})\times(d_{1}+d_{2})}
where for
\mathbf{Y}\in\mathbb{C}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{Y}):=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}_{d_{1}\times d_{1}}&\mathbf{Y}\\
\mathbf{Y}^{\dagger}&\mathbf{0}_{d_{2}\times d_{2}}\end{pmatrix}.

Its usage is usually in assessing properties of the singular values of the input matrix through its spectrum, since

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{Y})^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{Y}^{\dagger}&\mathbf{0}_{d_{1}\times d_{2}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{d_{2}\times d_{1}}&\mathbf{Y}^{\dagger}\mathbf{Y}\end{pmatrix}.

The most important properties of this operator is that it is real-linear, self-adjoint, and it preserves the spectral norm of the input
[
192
]
, in other words

\rho(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{Y}))=\left\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{Y})\right\|_{2}=\left\|\mathbf{Y}\right\|_{2}

(6.22)

Through this, then the following series of (in)equalities is true for each
j=0,1,\ldots,J
:

\mathbb{P}(B_{j}(\Delta t))=\mathbb{P}\big(\big\|\mathbf{H}^{j}\Delta t\big\|_{2}\geq 1\big)=\mathbb{P}\left(\big\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{H}^{j})\big\|_{2}\geq\frac{1}{\Delta t}\right)\leq\underset{\theta>0}{\mathrm{inf}}\left\{e^{-\theta/\Delta t}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{tr}\left(e^{\theta\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{H}^{j})}\right)\right]\right\},

(6.23)

where the inequality used at the end follows from the Laplace transform method, which provides tail bounds for the extreme eigenvalues of self-adjoint matrices
[
194
,
195
,
170
]
, which
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{H}^{j})
is by definition (even if
\mathbf{H}^{j}
is not). We now isolate the quantity
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{tr}\left(e^{\theta\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{H}^{j})}\right)\right]
for some
\theta>0
. Now, generally
\lambda(e^{\theta\mathbf{V}})=e^{\lambda(\theta\mathbf{V})}\pmod{2\pi i}=e^{\theta\lambda(\mathbf{V})}\pmod{2\pi i}
for arbitrary
\mathbf{V}
, but
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{H}^{j})
is self-adjoint, and specifically nonnegative-definite, and as such has real and nonnegative eigenvalues which means this relation is exact, or
\lambda(e^{\theta\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{H}^{j})})=e^{\theta\lambda(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{H}^{j}))}
. Now, by block-matrix algebra, we have
\mathrm{det}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{H}^{j})-\lambda\mathbf{I}_{2l})=\mathrm{det}(\lambda^{2}\mathbf{I}_{l}-\mathbf{H}^{j}(\mathbf{H}^{j})^{\dagger})
, which shows that the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{H}^{j})
are exactly the square roots of the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{H}^{j}(\mathbf{H}^{j})^{\dagger}
(or, equivalently,
(\mathbf{H}^{j})^{\dagger}\mathbf{H}^{j}
), which are exactly the singular values of
\mathbf{H}^{j}
. As such, by combining the preceding observations and using the linearity of the trace and expectation, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{tr}\big(e^{\theta\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{H}^{j})}\big)\right]=\sum_{\mathclap{\mu\in\lambda((\mathbf{H}^{j})^{\dagger}\mathbf{H}^{j})}}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\theta\sqrt{\mu}}\right].

(6.24)

We now bound the eigenvalues of
(\mathbf{H}^{j})^{\dagger}\mathbf{H}^{j}
. It is known from a multiplicative analog of the fundamental Lidskii-Mirsky-Wielandt eigenvalue majorization theorem that for
\mu_{1}(\mathbf{V})\geq\mu_{2}(\mathbf{V})\geq\cdots\geq\mu_{p}(\mathbf{V})
denoting the ordered eigenvalues of some Hermitian matrix
\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{C}^{p\times p}
then we have
[
161
]

\mu_{i}(\mathbf{S}^{\dagger}\mathbf{V}\mathbf{S})\leq\rho(\mathbf{S}^{\dagger}\mathbf{S})\mu_{i}(\mathbf{V}),\ \forall i=1,\ldots,p,

with
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{C}^{p\times p}
arbitrary. Using then this result on
(\mathbf{H}^{j})^{\dagger}\mathbf{H}^{j}=\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j})^{2}\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1}
, we have

\mu_{i}((\mathbf{H}^{j})^{\dagger}\mathbf{H}^{j})\leq\rho\big(\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1}\big)^{2}\mu_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j})^{2},\ \forall i=1,\ldots,l,

(6.25)

since
\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1}
is Hermitian and for
p\in\mathbb{C}_{m}[z]
a polynomial we have
p(\lambda(\mathbf{V}))=\lambda(p(\mathbf{V}))
in
\mathbb{C}
. We have already shown during the proof of Theorem
2.2
that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.
\rho\big(\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1}\big)<+\infty
uniformly in time, with a similar result for the filter covariance matrix itself being true too
[
126
,
152
,
154
]
. So, here we aim to bound
\lambda(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j})
for each
j=0,1,\ldots,J
. For that, the following series of (in)equalities hold for any
\tilde{\mu}\in\lambda(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j})
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{\mu}&\leq\rho(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j})=\big\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j}\big\|_{2}\\
&\leq 2\big\|\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big\|_{2}\big\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|_{2}+\big\|(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j}\big\|_{2}.\end{split}

(6.26)

But, as already mentioned, we have by our set of assumed regularity conditions that
\big\|\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big\|_{2}<+\infty
uniformly in time (
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.)
[
126
,
152
,
154
]
,
\big\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|_{2}<+\infty
(
\big\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|_{2}\leq l^{3/2}\big\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|_{\infty,\infty}\leq l^{3/2}L_{1}
by
(2)
), while for
\left\|(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j}\right\|_{2}
, we have by the sub-multiplicativity of the spectral norm that

\big\|(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j}\big\|_{2}\leq\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|^{2}_{2}+\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|^{2}_{2}.

As such, by combining these observations with (
6.26
) into (
6.25
), yields for all
i=1,\ldots,l

\mathbb{P}
-a.s. the following:

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mu_{i}((\mathbf{H}^{j})^{\dagger}\mathbf{H}^{j})}

\displaystyle\leq\rho\big(\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1}\big)\mu_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j})

\displaystyle\leq\big\|\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1}\big\|_{2}\big(2\big\|\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big\|_{2}\big\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|_{2}+\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|^{2}_{2}+\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|^{2}_{2}\big)

\displaystyle\leq M\big(1+\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|^{2}_{2}+\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|^{2}_{2}\big),

where the quantity
M
is defined as

M:=\underset{t\in[0,T]}{\sup}\big\{\big\|\mathbf{R}_{\text{f}}^{-1}(t)\big\|_{2}\big\}\max\Big\{2l^{3/2}L_{1}\underset{t\in[0,T]}{\sup}\big\{\big\|\mathbf{R}_{\text{f}}(t)\big\|_{2}\big\},1\Big\},

which is finite
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. Putting the preceding results back into (
6.24
), gives for
\Delta t>0
:

\displaystyle e^{-\theta/\Delta t}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{tr}\big(e^{\theta\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{H}^{j})}\big)\right]

\displaystyle=e^{-\theta/\Delta t}\sum_{\mathclap{\mu\in\lambda((\mathbf{H}^{j})^{\dagger}\mathbf{H}^{j})}}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\theta\sqrt{\mu}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq l\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\theta M\big(1+\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|^{2}_{2}+\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|^{2}_{2}\big)-\theta/\Delta t}\right]

\displaystyle\leq l\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\theta M\big(1+\underset{0\leq j\leq J}{\max}\big\{\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|^{2}_{2}+\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|^{2}_{2}\big\}\big)-\theta/\Delta t}\right],

and as such by (
6.23
) we have for any
j=0,1,\ldots,J
that

\mathbb{P}(B_{j}(\Delta t))\leq l\underset{\theta>0}{\inf}\Bigg\{\mathbb{E}\Bigg[e^{\theta M\big(1+\underset{0\leq j\leq J}{\max}\big\{\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|^{2}_{2}+\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|^{2}_{2}\big\}\big)-\theta/\Delta t}\Bigg]\Bigg\}.

Congregating all back into a probability result for
A(\Delta t)
and using
J=T/\Delta t
, retrieves

\mathbb{P}(A(\Delta t))\leq\sum_{j=0}^{J}\mathbb{P}(B_{j}(\Delta t))\leq l\frac{T+\Delta t}{\Delta t}a_{\Delta t}(M),

where
a_{\Delta t}(M)
is defined in assumption
(13)
found in the main text. As such, by condition
(13)
, we have that

0\leq\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}(A(\Delta t))\leq\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}l\frac{T+\Delta t}{\Delta t}o(\Delta t)=0,

which yields the desired result of
\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\left(\underset{0\leq j\leq J}{\max}\left\{\left\|\mathbf{H}^{j}\Delta t\right\|_{2}\right\}\geq 1\right)=0
.

The tail result concerning
\mathbf{L}
in (
6.21
) is obtained by following a similar procedure. Again, recall that in the proof of Theorem
2.2
it is shown that
\underset{t\in[0,T]}{\sup}\big\{\big\|\mathbf{R}_{\text{f}}(t)\big\|_{2}^{2}\big\},\underset{t\in[0,T]}{\sup}\big\{\big\|\mathbf{R}_{\text{f}}^{-1}(t)\big\|_{2}^{2}\big\}<+\infty
(
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.)
[
126
,
152
,
154
]
. Then, if we are to proceed in the same manner, we have an analogous inequality to (
6.25
) for any
i=1,\ldots,k
, by using similar algebraic manipulations and tools as the ones showcased for the tail bound concerning
\mathbf{H}
and in the proof of Theorem
2.2
:

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mu_{i}((\mathbf{L}^{j})^{\dagger}\mathbf{L}^{j})}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\rho\big(\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-2}\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\big)}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\mathrm{tr}\big(\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-2}\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\big)}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\mathrm{tr}\big(\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1/2}\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1/2}\big)\big\|\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1}\big\|_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{tr}\big(\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1/2}\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1/2}\big)

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\|(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1/2}\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\big\|^{2}_{2}\big\|\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1/2}\big\|^{2}_{2},

where we used the inequality
|\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{V}^{*}\mathbf{U})|\leq\big\|\mathbf{V}\big\|_{2}\mathrm{tr}\big((\mathbf{U}^{\dagger}\mathbf{U})^{1/2}\big)
for
\mathbf{V},\mathbf{U}\in\mathbb{C}^{p\times p}

[
196
]
and

(\mathbf{L}^{j})^{\dagger}\mathbf{L}^{j}=\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-2}\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}.

However, algebraic manipulations reveal that

\displaystyle\big\|(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1/2}\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\big\|^{2}_{2}\lesssim\big\|\big(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1/2}\big\|^{2}_{2}\big\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j}\big\|^{2}_{2}+\big\|\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1/2}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j}\big\|^{2}_{2}\big\|\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1}\big\|^{2}_{2},

\displaystyle\big\|\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1/2}\big\|^{2}_{2}\lesssim\big\|\big(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1/2}\big\|^{2}_{2}\big\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j}\big\|^{2}_{2}\big\|\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big\|^{2}_{2}+\big\|(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1/2}\big\|^{2}_{2},

where by
(5)
,
\big\|\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1}\big\|_{2}^{2}
is uniformly bounded in time (
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.), and so is
\big\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j}\big\|_{2}^{2}
by
(2)
, as well as
\big\|\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big\|_{2}^{2}
and
\big\|\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1}\big\|_{2}^{2}
as aforementioned, while
[
126
]

\big\|\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1/2}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j}\big\|^{2}_{2}=\big\|(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1/2}\big\|^{2}_{2}\lesssim\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|_{2}^{2}+\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|_{2}^{2}.

We then proceed in the same manner as in the preceding analysis as to deal with the term
\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|^{2}_{2}+\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|^{2}_{2}
under assumption
(13)
for an appropriate random scalar
\tilde{M}>0
, specifically to obtain a bound of the form

\sqrt{\mu_{i}((\mathbf{L}^{j})^{\dagger}\mathbf{L}^{j})}\leq\tilde{M}\big(1+\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|_{2}^{2}+\big\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{\mathbf{y},j}\big\|_{2}^{2}\big),

for each
i=1,\ldots,k
and
j=0,1,\ldots,J
, where now
\tilde{M}
depends on the uniform upper bounds, in time, of
\big\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}\big\|_{2}
,
\big\|\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}\big\|_{2}
,
\big\|\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}\big\|_{2}
, but also of
\big\|(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}\big\|_{2}
.
∎

The second lemma concerns the distributions of the backward conditionals of the unobserved process, as well as the Markov property that the partially observed system enjoys.

Lemma 6.6 (Distribution of Backwards Conditionals).

Let the assumptions in Theorem
2.3
hold. Then the conditional distributions
\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)
and
\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j+1\big)
are Gaussian,
\mathcal{N}_{l}(\mathbf{m}_{j}^{j},\mathbf{P}_{j}^{j})
and
\mathcal{N}_{l}(\mathbf{m}_{j+1}^{j},\mathbf{P}_{j+1}^{j})
, respectively, where the subscripts denote up to which observation we are conditioning on, while the superscripts denote the time instant we are currently on in terms of the unobservable. Their conditional means
\mathbf{m}_{r}^{j}
and conditional covariance matrices
\mathbf{P}_{r}^{j}
, for
r=j,j+1
, satisfy the following respective discrete-time equations:

\displaystyle\mathbf{m}_{j}^{j}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{C}_{j}^{j}(\mathbf{y}^{j+1}-(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t)\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}-\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t),

(6.27)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{j}^{j}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}-\mathbf{C}_{j}^{j}(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j})\Delta t)(\mathbf{C}_{j}^{j})^{\dagger},

(6.28)

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{m}_{j+1}^{j}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{C}_{j+1}^{j}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{y}^{j+1}-(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t)\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}-\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t\\
\mathbf{x}^{j+1}-\mathbf{x}^{j}-(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x},j})\Delta t\end{pmatrix},

(6.29)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{j+1}^{j}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}-\mathbf{C}_{j+1}^{j}\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}(\mathbf{C}_{j+1}^{j})^{\dagger},

(6.30)

where the auxiliary matrices
\mathbf{C}_{j}^{j}\in\mathbb{C}^{l\times l}
,
\mathbf{C}_{j+1}^{j}\in\mathbb{C}^{l\times(k+l)}
, and
\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\in\mathbb{C}^{(k+l)\times(k+l)}
, are defined by

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}_{j}^{j}:=\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t)^{\dagger}(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j})\Delta t)^{-1},

(6.31)

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}_{j+1}^{j}:=\mathbf{C}^{j}_{12}(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22})^{-1},\text{ where }\mathbf{C}^{j}_{12}:=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}\Delta t)&\hskip 3.99994pt\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\Delta t\end{pmatrix}

(6.32)

\displaystyle\text{and }\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}:=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j})\Delta t&(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j})\Delta t\\
(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j})\Delta t&(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\Delta t\end{pmatrix}.

(6.33)

Furthermore, if there is no noise cross-interaction term, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\equiv\mathbf{0}_{l\times k}
, then the following distributions are asymptotically equal as
\Delta t\to 0+
(or, equivalently, as
J\to\infty
):

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq J\big)=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big),

(6.34)

which means
\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1}
possesses the backwards in time Markov property of first-order w.r.t. natural filtration of
\mathbf{x}
, in continuous time, while it does so up to
O(\Delta t)
, or to leading-order, in discrete time. In general, when we allow for noise cross-interaction, we instead have the weaker Markov property of

\Big(\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)\neq\Big)\ \mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j+1\big)=\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq J\big),

(6.35)

which means the smoother update depends on the present as well as immediate “future” filter states, or that
\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1}
is a backwards in time second-order Markov chain w.r.t. natural filtration of
\mathbf{x}
, in continuous time (where in discrete time this equality is actually exact for all
j
and
\Delta t
).

Proof of Lemma
6.6
.

We start with the conditional distribution
\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)
. To do that, we shall first analyze the distribution
\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j},\mathbf{y}^{j+1}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)
. This distribution’s Gaussianity can be established by the decomposition

\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j},\mathbf{y}^{j+1}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)=\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j+1}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big),

and the enforcement of Lemma
6.3
, since each conditional on the right-hand side is Gaussian. Indeed, the first one is Gaussian as an immediate consequence of Lemma
6.2
and (
2.11
), while the second one is simply the Gaussian filter distribution. Specifically, in this case we let
\mathbf{u}_{2}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\mathbf{y}^{j+1}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j
and
\mathbf{u}_{1}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\mathbf{y}^{j}
in Lemma
6.3
, and observe how the mean of
\mathbf{y}^{j+1}
is indeed an affine transformation of
\mathbf{y}^{j}
, when conditioned on
\mathbf{y}^{j}
and
\mathbf{x}^{s}
for
s\leq j
, by virtue of (
2.11
). Otherwise, we can explicitly prove the conditional Gaussianity using the conditional characteristic function method
[
137
]
. As such we just need to find its Gaussian statistics to fully describe this distribution. We already know from (
6.5
) that

\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j+1}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}_{l}\big(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y},j})\Delta t,\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j})\Delta t\big),

while
\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)
is simply given by the filtering formula,

\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}_{l}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j},\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}).

As for the cross-covariance term, similarly to how we derived (
6.7
), by multiplying (
6.3
) by
(\mathbf{y}^{\prime,j})^{\dagger}
from the right-hand side and taking the average when conditioning on
\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j
, gives

\overline{\mathbf{y}^{\prime,j+1}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime,j})^{\dagger}}=(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t)\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}.

Therefore, by collecting all results thus far leads to

\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j},\mathbf{y}^{j+1}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}_{2l}\left(\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\\
(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t)\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}&\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t)^{\dagger}\\
(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t)\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}&\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\mathbf{H}^{j}\Delta t)\end{pmatrix}\right),

(6.36)

for
\mathbf{H}^{j}
as in the proof of Theorem
2.2
. Applying then Lemma
6.4
to the conclusion (
6.36
), yields the desired conditional distribution

\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}_{l}(\mathbf{m}_{j}^{j},\mathbf{P}_{j}^{j}),

where

\displaystyle\mathbf{m}_{j}^{j}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{C}_{j}^{j}(\mathbf{y}^{j+1}-(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t)\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}-\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t),

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{j}^{j}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}-\mathbf{C}_{j}^{j}(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j})\Delta t)(\mathbf{C}_{j}^{j})^{\dagger},

with the auxiliary matrix
\mathbf{C}_{j}^{j}
given by

\mathbf{C}_{j}^{j}:=\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t)^{\dagger}(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j})\Delta t)^{-1}=\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t)^{\dagger}(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\mathbf{H}^{j}\Delta t))^{-1}.

These establish (
6.27
)–(
6.28
) and (
6.31
).

We proceed with
\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j+1\big)
. For that, we first analyze
{\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j},\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{j+1}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)}
. The Gaussianity of this distribution, in a parallel manner as before, can be established by its definition,

\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j},\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{j+1}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)=\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{j+1}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big),

and enforcing Lemma
6.3
, since each conditional on the right-hand side is Gaussian, with the first being Gaussian as shown in the proof of Theorem 13.3 in Liptser & Shiryaev
[
69
]
while the second one is simply the filter posterior distribution which is Gaussian by Theorem
2.2
. To do so, in this case we let
\mathbf{u}_{2}=((\mathbf{y}^{j+1})^{\mathtt{T}},(\mathbf{x}^{j+1})^{\mathtt{T}})^{\mathtt{T}}
and
\mathbf{u}_{1}=\mathbf{y}^{j}
in Lemma
6.3
, and observe how the mean of
\mathbf{u}_{2}
is indeed an affine transformation of
\mathbf{u}_{1}=\mathbf{y}^{j}
, when conditioned on
\mathbf{y}^{j}
and
\mathbf{x}^{s}
for
s\leq j
, by virtue of (
2.10
)–(
2.11
). As such, having established the normality of this joint distribution, we just need to calculate its Gaussian moments. Marginally, we already know from the proof of Theorem
2.2
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}_{l}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j},\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j+1}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}_{l}\big(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y},j})\Delta t,\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j})\Delta t\big),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{x}^{j+1}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}_{k}\big(\mathbf{x}^{j}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x},j})\Delta t,(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\Delta t\big).

As for the cross-covariance terms, those are given by a similar procedure as before as

\overline{\mathbf{y}^{\prime,j+1}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime,j})^{\dagger}}=(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t)\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{\mathbf{x}^{\prime,j+1}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime,j})^{\dagger}}=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\Delta t.

We also have the conditionally Gaussian joint distribution
p(\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{j+1}|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j)
given in (
6.8
)–(
6.9
). As such, by gathering all of the established results, we end up with

\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j},\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{j+1}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}_{2l+k}\left(\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\\
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y},j}\right)\Delta t\\
\mathbf{x}^{j}+\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x},j}\right)\Delta t\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}&\mathbf{C}^{j}_{12}\\
\mathbf{C}^{j}_{21}&\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\end{pmatrix}\right),

(6.37)

where

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}^{j}_{12}:=\mathrm{Cov}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j},(\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{j+1})\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}\Delta t)&\hskip 3.99994pt\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\Delta t\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{C}^{j}_{21}:=(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{12})^{\dagger},

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}:=\mathrm{Var}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{j+1}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\Big(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\mathbf{H}^{j}\Delta t\Big)&\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\Delta t\\
\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\Delta t&(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\Delta t\end{pmatrix},

for the matrices
\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}
and
\mathbf{H}^{j}
as defined in the proof of Theorem
2.2
. Once again, we then utilize Lemma
6.4
to the conclusion in (
6.37
) as to retrieve the desired conditional distribution:

\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j+1\big)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}_{l}(\mathbf{m}_{j+1}^{j},\mathbf{P}_{j+1}^{j}),

where

\mathbf{m}_{j+1}^{j}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{C}_{j+1}^{j}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{y}^{j+1}-(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t)\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}-\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t\\
\mathbf{x}^{j+1}-\mathbf{x}^{j}-(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x},j})\Delta t\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{P}_{j+1}^{j}=\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}-\mathbf{C}_{j+1}^{j}(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{12})^{\dagger}=\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}-\mathbf{C}_{j+1}^{j}\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}(\mathbf{C}_{j+1}^{j})^{\dagger},

with the auxiliary matrix
\mathbf{C}_{j+1}^{j}
defined as
\mathbf{C}_{j+1}^{j}:=\mathbf{C}^{j}_{12}(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22})^{-1}
. These establish the rest of the relations, (
6.29
)–(
6.30
) and (
6.32
)–(
6.33
).

Having established (
6.27
)–(
6.33
), we now prove the statements concerning the Markov property of the backward conditionally Gaussian distributions of interest, (
6.34
) and (
6.35
). First, observe that for any
j=0,1,\ldots,J-1
, we have

\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq J\big)\equiv\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j+1\big).

(6.38)

This equality in distribution in (
6.38
) has the immediate consequence that
\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1}
is a backwards in time (the Markov property is intrinsically time-symmetric) second-order Markov chain w.r.t. natural filtration of
\mathbf{x}
, in discrete time for all
J\in\mathbb{N}
. This is in general true, due to the fact that the information of
\mathbf{x}^{s},s\geq j+1
is already included in the optimal filter estimated state of
\mathbf{y}^{j+1}
under our regime of working with the observational
\sigma
-algebra induced by
\{\mathbf{x}^{s}\}_{s=0,1,\ldots,j}
, which is the natural filtration of choice in partially observed stochastic dynamical systems, but also includes the uncertainty in
\mathbf{x}^{j+1}
. Essentially, when at the time step
t_{j}
, the conditional terms
\mathbf{y}^{j+1}
and
\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j+1
, resolve the information in the future and past, respectively, fully. To rigorously see why (
6.38
) is true, by (
2.10
)–(
2.11
), when conditioning on
\mathbf{y}^{j+1}
and
\mathbf{x}^{s}
for
s=0,1,\ldots,J
, by property of the filtration induced by the observational process, it is immediate that
\mathbf{y}^{j}
is fully determined by the information conditioned up to
t_{j+1}
, specifically by
\mathbf{y}^{j+1}
and
\mathbf{x}^{s}
for
s=0,1,\ldots,j+1
. Beyond this result though, it is possible to show that in the absence of noise cross-interaction, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\equiv\mathbf{0}_{l\times k}
, we actually have that

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq J\big)=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big),

or equivalently when
J\to\infty
, which is analogous to saying that
\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1}
possesses the (backwards in time) Markov property of first-order w.r.t. natural filtration of
\mathbf{x}
, in continuous-time (and up to
O(\Delta t)
, or to leading-order, in discrete-time). To contrast this with (
6.38
), when
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\not\equiv\mathbf{0}_{l\times k}
, then for the smoother update it is necessary to augment the information by
\mathbf{x}^{j+1}
(and only), since
\mathbf{y}^{j+1}
no longer contains all the information about the future states on its own. We mention that a similar result is shown in the proof of Theorem 13.11 (specifically (13.126)) of Liptser & Shiryaev
[
69
]
for the discrete-time case; specifically, under our notation, we have that
\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq J,\mathbf{y}^{N},\ldots,\mathbf{y}^{J}\big)=\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq N,\mathbf{y}^{N}\big)
, for
j<N\leq J
, which recovers (
6.38
) for
N=j+1
and
j+1=1,\ldots,J
. Here we instead prove the result in a martingale-free approach, and also obtain a pertinent condition for the continuous-time case in the process.

By virtue of block-matrix inversion through Schur complements, we see that if we define

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}&=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j})\Delta t&(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j})\Delta t\\
(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j})\Delta t&(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\Delta t\end{pmatrix}\\
&=:\begin{pmatrix}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)_{11}&\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)_{12}\\
\\
\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)^{\hskip 3.27773pt\mathtt{T}}_{12}&\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)_{22}\end{pmatrix},\end{split}

(6.39)

then

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22})^{-1}&=\begin{pmatrix}\left((\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22})^{-1}\right)_{11}&\left((\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22})^{-1}\right)_{12}\\
\left((\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22})^{-1}\right)_{21}&\left((\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22})^{-1}\right)_{22}\end{pmatrix}\\
&:=\begin{pmatrix}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)^{\hskip 3.27773pt-1}_{11}+\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)^{\hskip 3.27773pt-1}_{11}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)_{12}\mathbf{D}^{j}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)_{12}^{\hskip 3.27773pt\mathtt{T}}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)^{\hskip 3.27773pt-1}_{11}&\hskip 14.22636pt-\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)^{\hskip 3.27773pt-1}_{11}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)_{12}\mathbf{D}^{j}\\
\\
-\mathbf{D}^{j}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)_{12}^{\hskip 3.27773pt\mathtt{T}}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)^{\hskip 3.27773pt-1}_{11}&\mathbf{D}^{j}\end{pmatrix},\end{split}

(6.40)

where

\mathbf{D}^{j}:=\left(\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)_{22}-\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)^{\hskip 3.27773pt\mathtt{T}}_{12}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)^{\hskip 3.27773pt-1}_{11}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)_{12}\right)^{-1},

(6.41)

is the inverse of the Schur complement of
\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)_{11}
in
\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}
(which is Hermitian and positive-definite since under our assumptions so is
\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}
). Since
\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq J\big)\equiv\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j+1\big)
always holds, if we were to assume
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\equiv\mathbf{0}_{l\times k}
, then to establish that in this regime the process
\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1}
enjoys the first-order Markov property in continuous-time we just need to prove

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j+1\big).

Based on the expressions we have in (
6.27
)–(
6.33
) and due to the conditional normality of the distributions, we would just need to establish equality of the statistics up to the second-order moment, i.e., we would just need to prove that

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}\mathbf{m}^{j}_{j}=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}\mathbf{m}^{j}_{j+1}\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}\mathbf{P}^{j}_{j}=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}\mathbf{P}^{j}_{j+1},

or, equivalently and respectively, that

\displaystyle\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}\mathbf{C}_{j}^{j}(\mathbf{y}^{j+1}-(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t)\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}-\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t)=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}\mathbf{C}_{j+1}^{j}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{y}^{j+1}-(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t)\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}-\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t\\
\mathbf{x}^{j+1}-\mathbf{x}^{j}-(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x},j})\Delta t\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}\mathbf{C}_{j}^{j}\left(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j}\right)\Delta t\right)(\mathbf{C}_{j}^{j})^{\dagger}=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}\mathbf{C}_{j+1}^{j}\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}(\mathbf{C}_{j+1}^{j})^{\dagger}\left(=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}\mathbf{C}_{j+1}^{j}(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{12})^{\dagger}\right).

We first expand and simplify, up to first-order terms (i.e., up to
O(\Delta t)
), the expressions of all the auxiliary matrices appearing in (
6.31
), (
6.32
), and (
6.39
)–(
6.41
). These are not only needed for the proof of the Markovian properties here, but also required to prove the expressions of the discrete smoother mean and covariance matrix (see Lemma
6.7
). We start by expanding the auxiliary matrix
\mathbf{C}_{j}^{j}
, where after suppressing terms of order higher than
O(\Delta t)
when carrying out the series expansion, it is equivalently given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{C}_{j}^{j}&=\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t)^{\dagger}\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j})\Delta t\big)^{-1}\\
&=\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t)^{\dagger}\left[\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j})^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j})\Delta t\big)\right]^{-1}\\
&=\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}\Delta t)\big(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j})\Delta t\big)^{-1}\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1}\\
&=\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}\Delta t)\big(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}-\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j})\Delta t+O(\Delta t^{2})\big)\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1}\\
&=\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}\Delta t-\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j})\Delta t+O(\Delta t^{2})\big)\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1}\\
&=\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}\Delta t-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\Delta t-\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}\Delta t-(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j}\Delta t+O(\Delta t^{2})\big)\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1}\\
&=\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\Delta t-(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j}\Delta t+O(\Delta t^{2})\big)\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1}\\
&=\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}-\big(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j}\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1}\big)\Delta t+O(\Delta t^{2}),\end{split}

(6.42)

where we have used Lemma
6.5
, which concerns the spectrum of
\mathbf{H}
, as to establish the (
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.) leading-order expansion of the inverse
(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\mathbf{H}^{j}\Delta t)^{-1}
through the Neumann series of
-\mathbf{H}^{j}\Delta t
, for sufficiently small
\Delta t>0

[
197
,
198
,
199
]
. As such, from (
6.42
), for
\mathbf{C}^{j}_{j}
we have that

\mathbf{C}^{j}_{j}=\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}-\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t+O(\Delta t^{2}),

where
\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{y},j}:=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j}\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1}
.

Based on the definitions of the auxiliary matrices defined in (
6.39
), it is immediate that

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)_{11}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j})\Delta t\equiv\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\mathbf{H}^{j}\Delta t),

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)^{\hskip 3.27773pt\mathtt{T}}_{12}

\displaystyle:=(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j})\Delta t\equiv\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\Delta t.

Using then the same procedure as in (
6.42
), we then have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)^{\hskip 3.27773pt\mathtt{T}}_{12}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)^{\hskip 3.27773pt-1}_{11}&=\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\left(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\mathbf{H}^{j}\Delta t\right)^{-1}\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1}\Delta t\\
&=\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\left(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}-\mathbf{H}^{j}\Delta t+O(\Delta t^{2})\right)\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1}\Delta t\\
&=\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\Delta t-\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\mathbf{H}^{j}\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1}\Delta t^{2}+O(\Delta t^{3}),\end{split}

(6.43)

and

\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)^{\hskip 3.27773pt\mathtt{T}}_{12}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)^{\hskip 3.27773pt-1}_{11}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)_{12}=\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\Delta t^{2}+O(\Delta t^{3}),

(6.44)

where we have again used Lemma
6.5
. While our scheme only considers first-order terms as of leading-order, we nevertheless explicitly state the form of the quadratic terms in
\Delta t
for these auxiliary matrices, as they are needed in the subsequent analysis. Before proceeding, we define for notational simplicity the auxiliary matrix
\mathbf{K}^{j}:=\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}
. For
\mathbf{D}^{j}
, which as aforementioned is the inverse of the Schur complement of
\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)_{11}
in
(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22})
, we then have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{D}^{j}&:=\left(\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)_{22}-\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)^{\hskip 3.27773pt\mathtt{T}}_{12}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)^{\hskip 3.27773pt-1}_{11}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)_{12}\right)^{-1}\\
&=\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\Delta t-\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\Delta t^{2}\big)^{-1}\\
&=\big(\mathbf{I}_{k\times k}-\underbrace{\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}}_{\displaystyle\mathclap{=\mathbf{K}^{j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\equiv\mathbf{L}^{j}}}\Delta t\big)^{-1}\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1}/\Delta t\\
&=\big(\mathbf{I}_{k\times k}+\mathbf{L}^{j}\Delta t-(\mathbf{L}^{j})^{2}\Delta t^{2}+O(\Delta t^{3})\big)\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1}/\Delta t\\
&=\left(\frac{1}{\Delta t}\mathbf{I}_{k\times k}+\mathbf{L}^{j}-(\mathbf{L}^{j})^{2}\Delta t+O(\Delta t^{2})\right)\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1}\\
&=\frac{1}{\Delta t}\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1}+\mathbf{K}^{j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{K}^{j})^{\dagger}+\mathbf{L}^{j}\mathbf{K}^{j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{K}^{j})^{\dagger}\Delta t+O(\Delta t^{2}),\end{split}

(6.45)

where we have used Lemma
6.5
, which concerns the spectral properties of
\mathbf{L}
, as to establish the (
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.) leading-order expansion of the inverse
(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}-\mathbf{L}^{j}\Delta t)^{-1}
through the Neumann series of
\mathbf{L}^{j}\Delta t
, for sufficiently small
\Delta t>0
, while for the last equality we used that
\mathbf{L}^{j}\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1}=\mathbf{K}^{j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{K}^{j})^{\dagger}
. Notice that since
\mathbf{L}^{j}\mathbf{K}^{j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{K}^{j})^{\dagger}=\mathbf{Q}^{j}\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1}(\mathbf{Q}^{j})^{\dagger}
, for
\mathbf{Q}^{j}=\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}
, then the symmetry of
\mathbf{D}^{j}
(as the Schur complement of a covariance matrix it is Hermitian and positive-definite) is also apparent by its leading-order expansion. By combining the expressions we have derived thus far, we end up with

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{D}^{j}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)^{\hskip 3.27773pt\mathtt{T}}_{12}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)^{\hskip 3.27773pt-1}_{11}&=\left(\frac{1}{\Delta t}\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1}+\mathbf{K}^{j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{K}^{j})^{\dagger}+O(\Delta t)\right)\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\times\big(\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\Delta t-\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\mathbf{H}^{j}\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1}\Delta t^{2}+O(\Delta t^{3})\big)\\
&=\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt+\big(\mathbf{K}^{j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{K}^{j})^{\dagger}\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}-(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j})^{-1}\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\mathbf{H}^{j}\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1}\big)\Delta t+O(\Delta t^{2})\\
&=\mathbf{K}^{j}+\big(\mathbf{K}^{j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{K}^{j})^{\dagger}\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}-\mathbf{K}^{j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\mathbf{H}^{j}\big(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\big)^{-1}\big)\Delta t+O(\Delta t^{2}).\end{split}

(6.46)

As such, when we assume
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\equiv\mathbf{0}_{l\times k}
, and if in block-matrix form we have

\mathbf{C}_{j+1}^{j}=\mathbf{C}^{j}_{12}(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22})^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{E}^{j}&\hskip 3.99994pt\mathbf{F}^{j}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{C}^{l\times(l+k)},

(6.47)

for
\mathbf{E}^{j}\in\mathbb{C}^{l\times l}
and
\mathbf{F}^{j}\in\mathbb{C}^{l\times k}
, then by block-matrix multiplication we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{E}^{j}&=\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}\Delta t)\left[\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)^{\hskip 3.27773pt-1}_{11}+\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)^{\hskip 3.27773pt-1}_{11}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)_{12}\mathbf{D}^{j}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)_{12}^{\hskip 3.27773pt\mathtt{T}}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)^{\hskip 3.27773pt-1}_{11}\right]\\
&\hskip 42.67912pt-\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\mathbf{D}^{j}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)_{12}^{\hskip 3.27773pt\mathtt{T}}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)^{\hskip 3.27773pt-1}_{11}\Delta t\\
&=\mathbf{C}^{j}_{j}+\mathbf{C}^{j}_{j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\mathbf{D}^{j}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)_{12}^{\hskip 3.27773pt\mathtt{T}}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)^{\hskip 3.27773pt-1}_{11}\Delta t-\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\mathbf{D}^{j}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)_{12}^{\hskip 3.27773pt\mathtt{T}}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)^{\hskip 3.27773pt-1}_{11}\Delta t\\
&=\mathbf{C}^{j}_{j}+\big(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{j}-\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}\big)\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\mathbf{D}^{j}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)_{12}^{\hskip 3.27773pt\mathtt{T}}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)^{\hskip 3.27773pt-1}_{11}\Delta t\\
&=\mathbf{C}^{j}_{j}+O(\Delta t^{2})\equiv\mathbf{C}^{j}_{j},\end{split}

(6.48)

where we have used (
6.42
) and (
6.46
) and suppressed higher order terms, while

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{F}^{j}&=-\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}\Delta t)\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)^{\hskip 3.27773pt-1}_{11}\left(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\right)_{12}\mathbf{D}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\mathbf{D}^{j}\Delta t\\
&=-\mathbf{C}^{j}_{j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\mathbf{D}^{j}\Delta t+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\mathbf{D}^{j}\Delta t\\
&=-\big(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{j}-\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}\big)\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\mathbf{D}^{j}\Delta t\\
&=\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\big(\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1}+\mathbf{K}^{j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\mathbf{K}^{j})^{\dagger}\Delta t+O(\Delta t^{2})\big)\\
&=\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j})^{\dagger}\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1}\Delta t+O(\Delta t^{2})\\
&\to\mathbf{0}_{l\times k},\text{ as }\Delta t\to 0^{+},\end{split}

(6.49)

where again we have used (
6.42
) and (
6.45
), and suppressed higher order terms. We point to the proof of Lemma
6.7
for the general form of
\mathbf{E}^{j}
and
\mathbf{F}^{j}
up to first-order terms when
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\not\equiv\mathbf{0}_{l\times k}
; see (
6.54
) and (
6.55
), respectively.

As such, it is immediate from the everything thus far that if
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\equiv\mathbf{0}_{l\times k}
, then up to order
O(\Delta t)
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left(\mathbf{m}^{j}_{j+1}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}=\right)&\mathbf{C}_{j+1}^{j}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{y}^{j+1}-(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t)\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}-\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t\\
\mathbf{x}^{j+1}-\mathbf{x}^{j}-(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x},j})\Delta t\end{pmatrix}\\
&=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{E}^{j}&\hskip 3.99994pt\mathbf{F}^{j}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{y}^{j+1}-(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t)\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}-\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t\\
\mathbf{x}^{j+1}-\mathbf{x}^{j}-(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x},j})\Delta t\end{pmatrix}\\
&=\mathbf{C}_{j}^{j}(\mathbf{y}^{j+1}-(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t)\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}-\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t)+O(\Delta t^{3/2})+O(\Delta t^{2})\\
&\equiv\mathbf{C}_{j}^{j}(\mathbf{y}^{j+1}-(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t)\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}-\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t)\left(=\mathbf{m}^{j}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\right),\end{split}

(6.50)

where we used (
6.47
)–(
6.49
) and (
2.10
), which makes the equality in the mean true up to order
O(\Delta t)
in discrete time, as well as in the limit
\Delta t\to 0^{+}
for continuous time, and

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}-\mathbf{P}^{j}_{j+1}=\mathbf{C}_{j+1}^{j}(\mathbf{C}^{j}_{12})^{\dagger}=\right)

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}_{j+1}^{j}\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}(\mathbf{C}_{j+1}^{j})^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{C}_{j}^{j}&\hskip 3.99994ptO(\Delta t)\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}\begin{pmatrix}(\mathbf{C}_{j}^{j})^{\dagger}\\
O(\Delta t)\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{C}_{j}^{j}\left(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j}\right)\Delta t\right)(\mathbf{C}_{j}^{j})^{\dagger}+O(\Delta t^{2})

(6.51)

\displaystyle\equiv\mathbf{C}_{j}^{j}\left(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}})^{j}\right)\Delta t\right)(\mathbf{C}_{j}^{j})^{\dagger}\left(=\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}-\mathbf{P}^{j}_{j}\right),

where we again used (
6.47
)–(
6.49
) as well as (
6.39
), which makes the equality in the covariance true up to order
O(\Delta t)
in discrete time, as well as in the limit
\Delta t\to 0^{+}
for continuous time.

Combining (
6.50
) and (
6.51
), are then enough to yield the desired result in continuous time,

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j\big)=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j+1\big)\left(=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq J\big)\right),

due to the conditional Gaussianity of these distributions. This ends the proof of Lemma
6.6
.
∎

Here we make a note about the result concerning the Markov property of
\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1}
with respect to natural filtration of
\mathbf{x}
, i.e., the fact that in general we have

p(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j+1)=p(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq J)\neq p(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j),\ \forall j=0,1,\ldots,J-1,

but in the absence of noise cross-interaction we have

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}p(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j+1)=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}p(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{y}^{j+1},\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq j),

with equality up to
O(\Delta t)
in the discrete-time case. The condition
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\equiv\mathbf{0}_{l\times k}
is only sufficient but not necessary for going from the second-order backwards in time Markov property w.r.t. the natural filtration of
\mathbf{x}
(in continuous-time) to the first-order one (i.e., the stronger property). Indeed, the algebra in the proof of Lemma
6.6
, as well as the results from Lemma
6.7
in the sequel, reveal that we may have the same result if instead we assume that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. over
[0,T]
we have:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}-(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x}})\mathrm{d}t\in\mathrm{Ker}(\mathbf{F})\text{ and }\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x}}\equiv\mathbf{0}_{k\times l}\text{ for }\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}\mathbf{m}^{j}_{j}=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}\mathbf{m}^{j}_{j+1},

\displaystyle\text{and }\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}))\mathbf{E}^{\dagger}+\mathbf{E}(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}})^{\dagger}+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}))\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}+\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\equiv\mathbf{0}_{l\times l}\text{ for }\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}\mathbf{P}^{j}_{j}=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta t\to 0^{+}\\
J\Delta t=T\end{subarray}}\mathbf{P}^{j}_{j+1},

or any other equivalent (to these) relations.

The third lemma concerns itself with the discrete equations of the optimal nonlinear smoother, derived using the discrete smoother distribution.

Lemma 6.7 (Discrete Optimal Nonlinear Smoother).

Let the assumptions in Theorem
2.3
hold. Denote by
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}^{j,J}
and
\mathbf{R}_{\text{{s}}}^{j,J}
the conditional mean and conditional covariance of the conditional distribution of the discrete smoother
\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq J\big)
. In other words,

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}^{j,J}=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq J\right],\quad\mathbf{R}_{\text{{s}}}^{j,J}=\mathrm{Cov}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j},\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq J\big)=\mathbb{E}\left[(\mathbf{y}^{j}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}^{j,J})(\mathbf{y}^{j}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}^{j,J})^{\dagger}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s\leq J\right].

Given a realization of the observable
\mathbf{x}^{j}
for
j=0,\ldots,J
, the optimal smoother estimate
\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s=0,\ldots,J\big)
is conditionally Gaussian,

\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{y}^{j}\big|\mathbf{x}^{s},s=0,\ldots,J\big)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}_{l}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}^{j,J},\mathbf{R}_{\text{{s}}}^{j,J}),

where the conditional mean
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}^{j,J}
and conditional covariance
\mathbf{R}_{\text{{s}}}^{j,J}
of the smoother at time step
t_{j}
satisfy the following (backward) recursive equations:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}^{j,J}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{E}^{j}\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}^{j+1,J}-(\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t)\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}-\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t\right)+\mathbf{F}^{j}\left(\mathbf{x}^{j+1}-\mathbf{x}^{j}-(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x},j})\Delta t\right),

(6.52)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{s}}}^{j,J}&=\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}-\mathbf{C}_{j+1}^{j}\mathbf{C}^{j}_{22}(\mathbf{C}_{j+1}^{j})^{\dagger}+\mathbf{E}^{j}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{s}}}^{j+1,J}(\mathbf{E}^{j})^{\dagger}\\
