\displaystyle=-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\Delta t-\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t+\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{y},j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\Delta t

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\Delta t,

and

(\mathbf{E}^{j}-\mathbf{I}_{l\times l})\mathbf{y}^{j+1}=((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}-\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{y},j})\mathbf{y}^{j+1}\Delta t.

Adding these last two term then gives

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+(\mathbf{E}^{j}-\mathbf{I}_{l\times l})\mathbf{y}^{j+1}-\mathbf{E}^{j}\big((\mathbf{I}_{l\times l}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t)\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\big(\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{y},j}-(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{x},j}\big)(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}-\mathbf{y}^{j+1})\Delta t-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\Delta t-\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=(\mathbf{B}^{j}(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j})^{-1}+\mathbf{A}^{j})(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}-\mathbf{y}^{j+1})\Delta t-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\Delta t-\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t.

By then summing up these results we recover

\displaystyle\mathbf{m}_{j+1}^{j}-\mathbf{y}^{j+1}

\displaystyle=-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y},j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}\Delta t-\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y},j}\Delta t+(\mathbf{B}^{j}(\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{j})^{-1}+\mathbf{A}^{j})(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}-\mathbf{y}^{j+1})\Delta t

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big((\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{j}\big)^{-1}\big(\mathbf{x}^{j}-\mathbf{x}^{j+1}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x},j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}^{j}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x},j})\Delta t\big),

and so by combining all of these and taking the limit
\Delta t\to 0^{+}
, leads to

\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}}=(-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{y}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}-\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{y}}+(\mathbf{B}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}+\mathbf{A})(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}-\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}))\mathrm{d}t+(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{y}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathbf{x}})^{-1}\big(\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}+(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{x}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{f}}}+\mathbf{f}^{\mathbf{x}})\mathrm{d}t\big)+\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_{\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}},

which is exactly what we wanted to prove.
∎

6.7
Proof of Theorem
3.4

Proof of Theorem
3.4
.

In this proof, the overline notation denotes taking the expectation of the quantity in question conditioned on the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{T}^{\mathbf{x}}
. Furthermore, since we are conditioning on the whole observational period, intricacies like those that appeared in the proof of Theorem
3.2
should not arise here. We plug-in the mean-fluctuation decomposition (
6.1
) into (
3.7
), or equivalently by linearity, take the ensemble average of (
3.8
) conditioned on the complete observational
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{T}^{\mathbf{x}}
, to get

\overline{\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\hat{\mathbf{y}}}}_{\text{s}}=\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}}-(\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{B}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1})(\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\text{s}}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}})\mathrm{d}t,

(6.66)

since
\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}\big|\mathcal{F}_{T}^{\mathbf{x}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{y}(t)\big|\mathcal{F}_{T}^{\mathbf{x}}\right]\big|\mathcal{F}_{T}^{\mathbf{x}}\right]=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}
, by the stability property of conditional expectations, and
\mathbf{W}_{\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{{s}}}
is a zero expectation source term.

Now, in a similar argument to the one found in the proof of Theorem
3.2
, if
\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\text{s}}
coincides with the smoother mean at the endpoint
t=T
, then by uniqueness of the solution to (
6.66
) (again under all the underlying assumptions considered thus far), they should do so at all time instants, regardless of whether we are talking about its weak or strong solution. Note here that unlike in the filter-based sampling strategy (see proof of Theorem
3.2
), where it was possible to obtain exponentially fast converging consistency even in the case of incorrect or misidentified initial conditions, here it is required for the consistency to hold that the backward samples match the smoother mean at
t=T
, which in turn matches the filter mean. This is because now time moves backwards, so in the case of misidentified “terminal” conditions, i.e.,
\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\text{s}}\neq\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}
at the end point
t=T
, even though there the filter and smoother statistics necessarily coincide, it could be that the relaxation to the smoother mean is slow as time “devolves” from
T
down to
0
, or equivalently as time runs backwards. Even though the assumption on the spectrum of
\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{B}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}
provides the exponentially mean-square stability of the smoother covariance statistics by the theory of backward random ordinary differential equations (see (
2.16
)), it might still fail to provide fast-converging consistency between the Gaussian statistics of the backward samples and smoother state estimation by virtue of the linearity in (
6.66
); this can also be observed by the behavior of the expected spectrum of the damping feedback
\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{B}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1}
in Figure
4.3
. This establishes
(a)
.

Now, subtracting (
6.66
) from (
3.8
) yields an SDE for the fluctuation part of
\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}
:

\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\hat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\text{s}}^{\prime}=-(\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{B}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1})(\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}})\mathrm{d}t+\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_{\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}}.

(6.67)

The covariance is given by
\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}^{\prime}\left(\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}^{\prime}\right)^{\dagger}}
, which by Itô’s lemma
[
122
,
124
,
125
,
163
,
203
]
and linearity, has a differential given by

\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\big(\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}^{\prime}\left(\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}^{\prime}\right)^{\dagger}}\big)}=\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}^{\prime}\Big(\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\hat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\text{s}}^{\prime}\Big)^{\dagger}}+\overline{\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\hat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\text{s}}^{\prime}\Big(\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}^{\prime}\Big)^{\dagger}}-\overline{\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\hat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\text{s}}^{\prime}\Big(\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\hat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\text{s}}^{\prime}\Big)^{\dagger}}.

(6.68)

As such, by plugging-in (
6.67
) into (
6.68
), similar to the proof of Theorem
3.2
, up to leading-order term
O(\mathrm{d}t)
we end up with

\overleftarrow{\mathrm{d}\big(\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}^{\prime}\left(\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}^{\prime}\right)^{\dagger}}\big)}=-\big((\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{B}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1})\mathbf{R}_{\text{{s}}}+\mathbf{R}_{\text{{s}}}(\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{B}\mathbf{R}_{\text{{f}}}^{-1})^{\dagger}-\mathbf{B}\big)\mathrm{d}t,

(6.69)

since we have established in the proof of
(a)
that
\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}}
necessarily, for all time instants
T\geq t\geq 0
if they match at the end point (or
\overline{\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}}
converges to the smoother mean as
t
decreases from
T
towards
0
exponentially fast, in the case where they differ at
t=T
), and so

\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}^{\prime}(\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}})^{\dagger}\Big|\mathcal{F}_{T}^{\mathbf{x}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[(\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{{s}}})\left(\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{\text{{s}}}^{\prime}\right)^{\dagger}\Big|\mathcal{F}_{T}^{\mathbf{x}}\right]=\mathbf{R}_{\text{{s}}},

with (
6.69
) being exactly the same as the smoother covariance equation given in (
2.16
). This establishes
(b)
, thus ending the proof.
∎
